L5: NDA:	208471
Submission Date(s):	July 27, 2015
Brand Name	(b) (4)
Generic Name	Lixisenatide injection
OCP Division	Clinical Pharmacology -2
OND division	Metabolism and Endocrinology Products
Sponsor	Sanofi-aventis U.S. LLC
Submission Type; Code	NDA 505(b)(1); Standard
Formulation; Strength(s)	Prefilled solution for subcutaneous injection:10-\mug/dose
20 \mug/dose

Proposed Indication	Indicated as an adjunct to diet and exercise to improve glycemic control in adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus

Dosage & Administration	Administer once daily within one hour prior to (b) (4) the first meal of the day (b) (4)
Inject subcutaneously in the abdomen, thigh or upper arm.
Initiate at 10 mcg once daily for 2 weeks. After 2 weeks, increase the dose to 20 mcg once daily.

Clinical Pharmacology Review Team	Suryanarayana Sista, Ritesh Jain, Nitin Mehrotra, Manoj Khirana (Acting)

L15: ## 1 Executive Summary
L16: Lixisenatide is being developed by Sanofi. Lixisenatide is a short-acting GLP-1 receptor agonist with affinity for the GLP-1 receptor, has structural similarities to exendin-4 and is claimed to be resistant to enzymatic cleavage by DPP-4. An NDA for lixisenatide was originally submitted on 20 December 2012 but was subsequently withdrawn on 10 September 2013 following discussions with the Agency regarding the proposed process for review of the interim data from a cardiovascular outcomes trial EFC11319 (ELIXA), that was ongoing at that time. The Clinical Pharmacology review of the Dec 2012 submission was already completed and posted in DARRTS. Lixisenatide NDA was re-submitted on 27 July, 2015. The Clinical Pharmacology review has been updated with additional information that the sponsor submitted. If approved, Lixisenatide will be the 5th approved drug in its class.
L17: Lixisenatide is intended to improve glycemic control in patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM).
L18: ## 1.1 Recommendation
L19: The Office of Clinical Pharmacology (OCP) has reviewed the clinical pharmacology data submitted on 07/27/2015 under NDA 208471 and recommend approval with the following recommendations.
L20: - OCP agrees with the sponsor proposed lixisenatide dosing regimen of 10 µg QD as the starting dose for 14 days followed by a maintenance dose of 20 µg from Day 15, to be administered within one hour prior to (b) (4) the first meal of the day (b) (4)
- OCP recommends no dose adjustment for lixisenatide in patients with mild and moderate renal impairment; however, use with caution is recommended. OCP recommends that dose of lixisenatide should be restricted to 10 µg QD in patients with severe renal impairment and should be increased to 20 µg QD only in patients tolerating lixisenatide who require additional glycemic control.
- OCP agrees with the sponsor proposed dosing recommendations for concomitant administration with warfarin, digoxin, and ramipril requiring no dose adjustment.
- In addition, OCP recommends thatoral medications that are dependent on threshold concentrations for efficacy, such as antibiotics, should be taken at least 1 hour before lixisenatide injection or with a meal or snack when lixisenatide is not administered
- patients taking oral contraceptives should be advised to take them at least 1 hour before lixisenatide administration or 11 hours after the morning dose of lixisenatide
- patients taking atorvastatin should be advised to take atorvastatin preferably 1 hour before lixisenatide administration
L21: A Required OCP Office Level briefing was held on August 12, 2013 (under NDA 204961) to discuss the review team's recommendations. Attendees included Drs. Chandrahas Sahajwalla, Issam Zineh, Shiew-Mei Huang, Darrell Abernethy, Mehul Mehta, Vikram Sinha, Suresh Doddapaneni, Brian Booth, Kellie Reynolds, and Anshu Marathe from OCP and Dr. Suchitra Balakrishnan from Office of New Drugs.
L22: ## 1.2 Phase IV Commitments
L23: None.
L24: ## 1.3 Summary of Important Clinical Pharmacology Findings
L25: The lixisenatide formulation is a clear, colorless solution (0.05 and 0.1 mg/mL lixisenatide solution) for subcutaneous injection, which is packaged in 3 mL cartridges fitted with (b) (4) closures. Each cartridge contains 0.15 or 0.3 mg of lixisenatide (calculated with reference to the active moiety).
L26: The proposed dosing regimen is that lixisenatide is administered once daily within one hour prior to (b) (4) the first meal of the day (b) (4) and can be injected subcutaneously in the abdomen, in the thigh or in the upper arm. For all patients lixisenatide should be initiated with a starting dose of 10 µg for 14 days, followed by a maintenance dose of 20 µg from Day 15.
L27: Key pharmacokinetic properties of lixisenatide are summarized in Table 1. Lixisenatide was interchangeably called as AVE0010 by the sponsor. Throughout this document, any reference to AVE0010 indicates that the drug being discussed is Lixisenatide.
L28: **Table 1 Highlights of Pharmacokinetics**
L29: Proposed dose	Starting dose of 10 µg for 14 days;
Maintenance dose of 20 µg from Day 15

Absorption	Median T_{max} – 1-3.5 h in patients with type 2 diabetes
Dose-proportional PK in the 10 µg-20 µg dose range
Absolute bioavailability reported to be about 32%
Unchanged lixisenatide is the main drug-related component in plasma

Distribution	Approximately 55% bound to human plasma protein
Mean Vz/F: Approximately 100L following subcutaneous administration, indicating extensive tissue distribution

Metabolism and Elimination	Lixisenatide, being a peptide, is eliminated through glomerular filtration, followed by tubular reabsorption and subsequent metabolic degradation, resulting in smaller peptides and amino acids, which are reintroduced in the protein metabolism.
Apparent terminal half-life: Approximately 3 hours after multiple-dose administration
Apparent clearance (CL/F) is about 35 L/h
L30: ## 1.3.1 Dose/exposure-response relationship for efficacy
L31: ## HbA1c:
L32: The sponsor studied only one dose of 20 µg QD in all the Phase 3 trials. Majority of the trough concentrations data in the Phase 3 trials were below the limit of quantification. Thus, dose/exposure response relationship for safety and efficacy is limited to data available from a single Phase 2 trial of 13-week duration (Study #DRI6012). In the Phase 2 study, a clear dose response relationship was observed, where increasing doses within the QD and BID-dosing regimen resulted in increase in response in terms of reduction in HbA1c (Figure 1), secondary efficacy parameter such as fasting plasma glucose and post prandial glucose, and weight loss also showed dose-response relationship. Compared to proposed 20 µg QD dosing, 10 µg BID-dosing regimen showed slight numerically greater reduction in HbA1c (-0.69 with 20 µg QD vs. -0.78 with 10 µg BID-dosing). Figure 2 shows cumulative distribution function (CDF) plots for change from baseline HbA1c between these two dosing regimens. Although a slight difference in mean change from baseline in HbA1c is seen in these two dosing group, overlapping CDF plots between 20 µg QD and 10 µg BID dosing indicates that the distribution for change from base line in HbA1C is similar across both the dosing groups.
L33: BID = twice daily; HbA1c = glycosylated hemoglobin; ITT = intent-to-treat; LS = least square; n = number of patients; QD = once daily
L34: **Figure 1 Mean change in HbA1c at endpoint (ITT population)**
L35: [Source: Sponsor's Summary of Clinical Efficacy, Figure 10, Page 131]
L36: **Figure 2 Cumulative distribution function plot for mean change from baseline in HbA1c at week 13 (Study DRI6012).**
L37: (AVE0010 10 µg BID = Lixisenatide 10 µg BID dosing; AVE0010 20 µg QD = Lixisenatide 20 µg QD dosing)
L38: ## Body Weight:
L39: A clear dose-response relationship for weight loss was observed for each dosing regimen, i.e., the higher the lixisenatide dose within a regimen (QD or BID) the larger the mean decrease in body weight. The data for mean change in weight (kg) from baseline to endpoint (week 13) is presented in Table 2. Compared to 10 µg BID dose, 20 µg QD dose had a numerically greater weight loss change.
L40: **Table 2** Mean change in weight (kg) from baseline to endpoint – ITT population in Phase 2 Trial (Study#DRI6012)

L42: ## 1.3.2 Dose/exposure-response relationship for Safety:
L43: The major safety issues associated with lixisenatide were gastrointestinal disorders such as nausea and vomiting. Majority of the trough concentrations data in the Phase 3 trials were below the limit of quantification. In addition, sponsor studied only one dose in all the Phase 3 trials. Thus, dose/exposure response relationship for safety is limited to data available from a single Phase 2 trial of 13-week duration (Study #DRI6012). In the Phase 2 study, a clear dose-response relationship with both regimens was seen in the number of subjects with GI related adverse events. The frequencies for GI side effects ranged from 21.8 % (5 µg) to 51.9 % (30 µg) in lixisenatide QD groups and from 22.6% (5 µg) to 57.4 % (30 µg) in lixisenatide BID groups. Doses higher than 20 µg in both QD and BID dosing regimen resulted in more gastrointestinal related adverse events (Table 3).
L44: Additionally, compared to 20 µg QD dosing regimen 10 µg BID dosing regimen had less gastrointestinal related side effects.

L47: ## 1.3.3 Benefit Risk Comparison of 20 µg QD and 10 µg BID dosing:
L48: Since results of the dose ranging study indicated that 10 µg BID dosing is better than 20 µg QD dosing in terms of efficacy and GI safety, we examined the benefit risk comparison of these two dosing regimen and agree with the sponsor's proposed dose of 20 µg QD for the following reasons:
L49: ## Efficacy
L50: - Compared to proposed 20 µg QD dosing, 10 µg BID-dosing regimen showed numerically slight higher reduction in HbA1c. However, the added benefit with 10 µg BID-dosing should be weighed against the narrow time window left for the administration of concomitant medication because of the effect of lixisenatide on delaying gastric emptying time.
L51: - Furthermore, Figure 2 shows that the cumulative distribution plots for change from baseline in HbA_{1c} is highly overlapped between these two dosing regimens indicating a similar overall distribution of change from baseline in HbA_{1c} in the two dosing groups.
- Compared to 10 \mug BID dose, 20 \mug QD dose showed a numerically greater reduction in weight.
L52: ## Safety
L53: - In the dose ranging Phase 2 study, doses higher than 20 \mug in both QD and BID dosing regimen resulted in more gastrointestinal related adverse events (Table 3).
- Additionally, compared to 20 \mug QD dosing regimen, 10 \mug BID dosing regimen had less gastrointestinal related side effects. However, as stated above the added safety benefit with BID dosing regimen needs to be counterbalanced with the inconvenience of co-administration of other drugs.
L54: ## Practicality
L55: - Lixisenatide delays the gastric emptying time and hence concomitant medications need to be administered (b) (4) 1 hour before lixisenatide administration or with next meal for those drugs that have to be administered with food.
- With BID dosing regimen, the subjects will have a narrow window for taking concomitant medications and hence we agree with the sponsor's proposed 20 \mug QD dosing regimen as acceptable from a practical standpoint.
L56: ## 1.3.4 *Intrinsic Factors*
L57: - Age: No clinically relevant effect on the pharmacokinetics of lixisenatide
- Gender: No clinically relevant effect on the pharmacokinetics of lixisenatide
- Race: No clinically relevant effect on the pharmacokinetics of lixisenatide
- Body weight: No clinically relevant effect on lixisenatide AUC
- Renal and Hepatic impairment: see below
L58: ## 1.3.5 *Drug-Drug Interactions:*
L59: - **Acetaminophen:** This drug interaction study was conducted to evaluate lixisenatide's effect on delaying GI motility. When administered 1 or 4 hours after 10 \mug lixisenatide, on average, AUC of acetaminophen was unaltered, C_{max} of acetaminophen was decreased by 29% and 31% respectively, and median t_{max} was delayed by 2.0 and 1.75 hours respectively. No dose adjustment for acetaminophen is recommended. This study clearly demonstrated that a 10 \mug dose of lixisenatide can have a delaying effect of up to 4 hours on absorption of drugs such as acetaminophen which are absorbed rapidly with a short t_{max} (1-3 hours). Sponsor did not conduct a study with the 20 \mug dose of lixisenatide. Oral medications that are dependent on threshold concentrations for efficacy, such as antibiotics, should be taken at least 1 hour before lixisenatide injection or with a meal or snack when lixisenatide is not administered.
- **Oral contraceptives:** Administration of an oral contraceptive 1 hour or 4 hours after lixisenatide did not affect AUC and t_{1/2} of ethinylestradiol and levonorgestrel, whereas C_{max} of ethinylestradiol was decreased by 52% and 39% respectively and C_{max} of levonorgestrel was decreased by 46% and 20%, respectively and median t_{max} was delayed by 1 to 3 hours. There was no change in AUC. The clinical implications of reduced peak exposure of ethinylestradiol and levonorgestrel when the oral contraceptive was administered 1 hour or 4 hours after lixisenatide injection, is unknown. It is quite possible that oral contraceptives depend on threshold concentrations for efficacy. Therefore, patients should be advised to
L60: take oral contraceptives at least 1 hour before lixisenatide administration or 11 hours after the morning dose of lixisenatide.
L61: - **Atorvastatin:** The steady-state C_{max} of atorvastatin was reduced by 31%, AUC was unaltered, and t_{max} was prolonged (with change in t_{max} ranging from -1 to 9 hours) when atorvastatin was administered in morning 1 hour after lixisenatide injection. However, when atorvastatin was administered in evening 12.5 hours following lixisenatide administration in the morning, C_{max} of atorvastatin was increased by 66%, AUC was increased by 27%, and t_{max} was unaltered (with change in t_{max} ranging from -3 to 2 hours). The 31% decrease in C_{max} observed for the morning dosing is not clinically relevant based on atorvastatin prescribing information; however, the significance of the effect of prolongation in t_{max} by up to 9 hours is not known. Although an increase in C_{max} and AUC was seen for evening administration, the increase in these parameters was only 25% and 18%, respectively, when comparing the atorvastatin evening administration following lixisenatide morning administration vs. atorvastatin administered alone in the morning. Since the safety and efficacy for atorvastatin administered alone in the morning is established, the 18-25% increase in exposure may not be clinically relevant. Further there were safety data available from completed Phase 3 trials for co-administration with statins. It showed that trends of treatment-emergent adverse events in the lixisenatide and placebo groups were not affected by concomitant statin use (85.3% in the lixisenatide group and 79.0% in the placebo group with statin use compared with 76.7% in the lixisenatide group and 65.2% in the placebo group without statin use). Based on safety information from Phase 3 trials, and information garnered from approved label for atorvastatin that LDL-C reduction is the same regardless of the time of day of drug administration, no dose adjustment is recommended for atorvastatin. However, because it may take longer to reach steady-state concentrations of atorvastatin when administered with lixisenatide, patients taking atorvastatin may be advised to take atorvastatin preferably 1 hour before lixisenatide administration.
- **Warfarin and other coumarin derivatives:** In this DDI study, warfarin was administered either in the morning on Day 1 of the treatment period without concomitant lixisenatide treatment (Treatment A), or on Day 11 of the treatment period with concomitant lixisenatide treatment (Treatment B). Lixisenatide was administered 30 minutes before breakfast while the single oral dose of 25 mg warfarin was administered with breakfast. While AUC did not change between warfarin alone and warfarin+lixisenatide treatments (ratio 1.03), C_{max} reduced 19% and 15% for S-warfarin and R-warfarin, respectively, when warfarin was administered after lixisenatide. There was, however, a shift in T_{max} from 1 hour post warfarin alone treatment to 8.0 hours post warfarin+lixisenatide treatment. The implication of the 7-hour delay in time to peak warfarin concentration is unknown. While no dose adjustment for warfarin is required when co-administered with lixisenatide based on lack of pharmacokinetic interaction, frequent monitoring of INR in patients on warfarin and/or coumarin derivatives, as well as following of instructions in the warfarin product monograph for appropriate monitoring and dose adjustment is recommended at the time of initiation or ending of lixisenatide treatment.
- **Digoxin:** When digoxin was administered 30 minutes post lixisenatide injection, the steady-state C_{max} of digoxin was reduced by 26% compared to digoxin alone. Steady-state AUC for digoxin remained unchanged between the treatments. T_{max} was prolonged from 0.5 hours with digoxin alone to 2 hours following digoxin given 30 minutes post lixisenatide injection. Due to the lixisenatide induced delay in time to peak digoxin concentrations, careful monitoring of digoxin levels are recommended. The health care provider should be cautioned to follow instructions in the product monograph for digoxin for appropriate dose adjustment, if necessary when administering with lixisenatide.
L62: - **Ramipril:** When ramipril was administered 30 minutes post lixisenatide injection, the steady-state C_{max} of ramipril was reduced by 73% compared to ramipril alone. Steady-state AUC for ramipril was increased by 21%. T_{max} was prolonged from 0.5 hours with ramipril alone to 2.8 hours following ramipril given 30 minutes post lixisenatide injection. For ramiprilat, the active moiety of ramipril, however, there was essentially no change in C_{max} (ratio 1.02) and AUC (ratio 1.11) between ramipril+lixisenatide and ramipril treatments. T_{max} for ramiprilat was prolonged from 2.5 hours with ramipril alone to 5.0 hours following ramipril given 30 minutes post lixisenatide injection. Mean trough concentrations of ramiprilat were similar between the two treatments (1.85 ng/mL following ramipril alone vs. 1.94 ng/mL following ramipril+lixisenatide). Ramipril is almost completely metabolized in the body to Ramiprilat, which has about 6 times the ACE inhibitory activity of Ramipril. Since the total exposure and trough concentrations of ramiprilat did not change following administration with lixisenatide compared to lixisenatide alone, no dose adjustment for ramipril is suggested when co administered with lixisenatide.
L63: ## 1.3.5.1 *Specific Population*
L64: ## 1.3.5.1.1 *Hepatic Impairment*
L65: No dosing adjustment is recommended in patients with hepatic impairment. This is similar to the dosing recommendation for other biologics.
L66: ## 1.3.5.1.2 *Renal Impairment*
L67: No dose adjustment is recommended in patients with mild renal impairment, however, use with caution is recommended because of the higher incidences of nausea, vomiting and percentage of hypoglycemic events observed in this population compared to normal subjects in the Phase 3 studies.
L68: There were no unique safety concerns observed in patients with moderate renal impairment. The sample size was small in the Phase 3 trials to draw any conclusions. However, 215 patients with moderate renal impaired were enrolled on lixisenatide in the dedicated CV safety trial (ELIXA trial), safety assessment of which did not show any unique safety concerns. Therefore, no dosing adjustment is recommended in patients with moderate renal impairment; however, use with caution is recommended because of general concerns of dehydration leading to acute renal failures with this class of drugs. Please refer to the clinical review by Dr. Suchitra Balakrishnan for complete details of the results for patients with moderate renal impairment from efficacy and safety trials.
L69: There were only 3 patients with severe renal impairment in the ELIXA trial. The sponsor proposed not to use lixisenatide in patients with severe renal impairment. However, given that efficacy of lixisenatide is preserved in patients with renal impairment and that there is no unique safety concerns in this population, this reviewer recommends that lixisenatide should be used with caution in patients with severe renal impairment. The dose of lixisenatide should be restricted to 10 \mug QD in patients with severe renal impairment, and should be increased to 20 \mug QD only in patients tolerating lixisenatide who require additional glycemic control.
L70: The recommendation for 10 \mug QD dose in patients with severe RI is based on exposure matching.
L71: ## 2 Question-Based Review (QBR)
L72: Lixisenatide NDA was originally submitted on 12/20/2012 under NDA 208471, and subsequently withdrawn in October 2013. The Clinical Pharmacology was completed and posted in DARRTS prior to the sponsor's withdrawal of the NDA. The NDA was re-submitted on 07/27/2015, under NDA 208471. Information from 4 new studies are available in this re-submission. Clinical Pharmacology findings from these studies are discussed in a separate addendum in Section 2.8.
L73: ## 2.1 What are the *in vitro* and *in vivo* Clinical Pharmacology and Biopharmaceutics studies and Clinical studies with PK and/or PD information submitted in the NDA
L74: The clinical pharmacology program performed to evaluate the pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic properties of lixisenatide included 26 clinical pharmacology trials. These comprised 17 Phase 1 trials in healthy subjects (including bioequivalence trials, trials in elderly subjects, subjects with renal impairment and Chinese subjects) and 5 trials (2 Phase 1 and 3 Phase 2) in subjects with type 2 diabetes (including one trial in Japanese subjects), and 3 Phase 3 studies from which PK data were available. The program was supported by 8 human biomaterial studies, which have been conducted to provide supporting information on the clinical pharmacology of lixisenatide (Table 4).
L75: These *in vitro* studies investigated protein binding, metabolic stability of lixisenatide in S9 liver and kidney fractions and identification of metabolites, the potential for lixisenatide to induce cytochrome P450 (CYP) isozyme activity in human hepatocytes or inhibit CYP isozyme activity in human liver microsomes, and the potential for lixisenatide to inhibit human organic cation transporter 2 (OCT2) (kidney) and organic anion transporting polypeptide 1B1 (OATP1B1) (liver) uptake transporters. A list of all completed clinical pharmacology studies is provided in Table 5.

L91: The clinical program performed to evaluate the efficacy and safety of lixisenatide included 14 Phase 3 trials of which 12 studies have been completed as of the NDA cut-off date of 01 June 2012 and 2 studies are ongoing. Change in HbA_{1c} was the primary endpoint in 9 double-blind placebo-controlled studies. In an active-controlled study conducted to support the claim of efficacy, the primary endpoint was a composite endpoint of reduction in both HbA_{1c} and body weight. This study was conducted in obese, T2DM, younger than 50 population. A listing of Phase 3 studies with lixisenatide is presented in Table 6.

L96: ## 2.2 General Attributes
L97: Lixisenatide is a human Glucagon-Like Peptide-1 (GLP-1) analog and acts as a GLP-1 receptor agonist. The GLP-1 receptor is the target for native GLP-1, an endogenous incretin hormone that potentiates glucose-dependent insulin secretion from the pancreatic beta cells.

L100: ## 2.2.2 What is the composition of to-be-marketed formulation of Lixisenatide?
L101: **Formulation:** Lixisenatide is a clear, colorless solution (0.05 and 0.1 mg/mL lixisenatide solution) for subcutaneous injection, which is packaged in 3 mL cartridges fitted with (b) (4) closures. Each cartridge contains 0.15 or 0.3 mg of lixisenatide (calculated with reference to the active moiety). The pH of the solution is about (b) (4). The solution contains the following excipients: water for injection, glycerol (85 per cent), sodium acetate trihydrate, methionine, metacresol, and hydrochloric acid/sodium hydroxide solution for pH adjustment. A prefilled green pen delivering 14 doses of 10 \mug and a prefilled (b) (4) pen delivering 14 doses of 20 \mug are available. Each green pre filled pen contains 3 mL solution and each mL contains 50 \mug (0.05 mg/mL) lixisenatide. Each (b) (4) pre filled pen contains 3 mL solution, and each mL contains 100 \mug (0.1 mg/mL) lixisenatide. The formulation is shown in Table 7.

L108: ## 2.2.3 What are the proposed mechanism of action and therapeutic indications?
L109: Glucagon-like peptide 1 is secreted from the L-cells of the lower gastrointestinal tract (mainly, from the ileum and colon) following the ingestion of a carbohydrate- or fat-containing meal. GLP-1 is known to regulate nutrient metabolism via stimulating effects on insulin release from the pancreatic islets (insulinotropic release), which also affects glucagon secretion (suppressive effect), gastric motility (slowing of gastric emptying), and stomach acid secretion.
L110: All these pharmacodynamic (PD) effects have been demonstrated in clinical studies employing exogenous GLP-1. The insulinotropic effect is glucose dependent, at glucose concentrations below approximately 4.5 mM, insulin secretion is not stimulated, thus minimizing the risk of relevant hypoglycemia. The slowing of gastric emptying together with a GLP-1 related increase in satiety (mediated through the central nervous system) may decrease body weight despite the insulinotropic effects.
L111: The active, circulating form of GLP-1 in humans is GLP-1 (7-36)-amide. GLP-1 has a very short half-life in circulation (&lt;2 minutes) mainly because of rapid N-terminal cleavage by the common, dipeptidyl peptidase-4 (DPP-4). Lixisenatide, an analog of exendin, a naturally occurring peptide is resistant to degradation by DPP-4.
L112: *In vitro* pharmacology studies in Chinese hamster ovary (CHO) cells transfected with the human GLP-1 receptor showed that lixisenatide had a binding affinity ~4 times greater than native human GLP-1. The IC50 of lixisenatide was 1.43 nmol/L vs. a GLP-1 IC50 of 5.48 nmol/L, which corresponds to Ki values of 1.33 and 5.09 nmol/L, respectively.
L113: Lixisenatide is indicated as an adjunct to diet and exercise to improve glycemic control in adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus.
L114: ## 2.2.4 What are the proposed dosages and routes of administration?
L115: Lixisenatide injection 10 µg and 20 µg to be administered subcutaneously are the proposed commercial strengths. The sponsor's proposed dosing recommendation is to start with a 10 µg once daily dose for 14 days followed by a 20 µg once-daily maintenance dose. Lixisenatide should be administered once daily
L116: within one hour prior to (b) (4) the first meal of the day (b) (4). If a dose is missed, liraglutide should be injected within the hour preceding the next meal.
L117: In addition, the following dosing recommendations are proposed by the sponsor:
L118: (b) (4)
L119: When liraglutide is added to a sulfonylurea or a basal insulin, there is a potential risk of hypoglycemia (based on results from studies EFC6016, EFC10887). A reduction of the concomitantly administered sulfonylurea or basal insulin may be necessary based on clinical experience.
L120: ## 2.3 General Clinical Pharmacology
L121: ## 2.3.1 What are the PK characteristics of liraglutide after subcutaneous administration and how do they relate to the dose?
L122: ## 2.3.1.1 Single Dose
L123: Single-dose pharmacokinetics of liraglutide in healthy subjects are available from 7 single dose studies across a dose range of 5 µg to 20 µg (studies BDR6864, BEQ11094, PDY11431, PDY11824, PDY11941, POP11814, and POP6053). Single dose data for the plasma PK of liraglutide in patients with T2DM are available from Study PDY10433 with a 20 µg dose, and from the first dose in Study PDY6797 (5 or 10 µg). Upon SC administration, maximum concentrations of liraglutide in systemic circulation were achieved between 1.5 - 3 hours in healthy subjects and between 1 – 3.5 hours in T2DM patients.
L124: A population PK analysis by the sponsor indicated that liraglutide has absorption-limited PK because the calculated population mean absorption time (MAT) of 3.25 hours was longer than the population mean elimination time (V/CL) of 1.06 hours (flip-flop kinetics). In healthy subjects as well as patients with T2DM, the extent of absorption (as reflected by the AUC and C_{max}) appeared to be less than dose proportional after single dose administration of liraglutide across dose levels of 5 to 20 µg. Further discussion is included in section 2.4.
L125: The reported *in vitro* plasma protein binding of liraglutide is low (~55%), and is largely independent of concentration. The volume of distribution in healthy subjects generally ranged between 60 and 120 L after single dosing, irrespective of the dose level administered, suggesting that liraglutide is distributed throughout the body. Similarly, in patients with T2DM, the volume of distribution ranged between approximately 70 and 140 L after single dosing, irrespective of the dose level administered.
L126: Across these dose groups, the elimination half-life was approximately 1.5 hours for healthy subjects as well as T2DM patients.
L127: Representative mean plasma concentration-time profiles of liraglutide following single-dose are illustrated in Figure 3 below for normal healthy volunteers and T2DM patients.

L141: ## 2.3.1.2 Multiple Once Daily Doses
L142: Multiple-dose pharmacokinetics of lixisenatide in healthy subjects are available from 8 multiple dose studies across a dose range of 10 µg QD to 30 µg BID (INT10408, INT10409, INT10782, INT10783, POP11320, TDR11215, TES6865, and TES11807). Since the presence of anti-lixisenatide antibodies increases the concentrations of total lixisenatide measured in plasma as well as the variability in derived PK parameters, the sponsor chose to describe the PK profile in healthy subjects after multiple dose administration of lixisenatide primarily by data from antibody-negative subjects. Multiple dose data for the plasma PK of lixisenatide in patients with T2DM are available from 3 studies across a dose range of 5 µg QD to 30 µg BID (ACT6011, DRI6012, PDY6797).
L143: After multiple dose administration, the extent of absorption was dose proportional in healthy subjects between dose levels of 10 and 20 µg QD, and in patients with T2DM between dose levels of 5 to 30 µg QD and BID.
L144: There was no clear relationship between lixisenatide dose levels and mean elimination half-life values. At steady state, the mean apparent total body clearance (CL/F) of lixisenatide ranged between 29 and 64 L/h in healthy subjects, and between 20 and 67 L/h in patients with T2DM, with no consistent trends across dose levels.
L145: Representative mean plasma concentration-time profiles of lixisenatide following multiple-dose are illustrated in Figure 4 below.

L167: ## 2.3.2 Based on PK parameters, what is the degree of the proportionality of the dose-concentration relationship?
L168: Pharmacokinetic evaluation following stepwise increases in lixisenatide of up to 20 µg QD or BID showed that the area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) and C_{max} increased with increasing doses of lixisenatide. In the anti-lixisenatide antibody-negative patients, dose-proportionality was seen within the QD and BID dosing regimens studied and the AUC value doubled for BID dosing as compared to QD dosing. The apparent terminal half-life (t_{1/2}) for lixisenatide was approximately 3 to 4 hours. In the anti-lixisenatide antibody-positive patients, the magnitude and variability of exposure increased and the elimination half-life was around 8 to 10 hours.
L169: Representative plot and data are shown in Figure 5 and Table 12, respectively.

L178: ## 2.3.3 What are the pharmacodynamic characteristics of lixisenatide after subcutaneous administration and how do they relate to the dose?
L179: Sponsor evaluated lixisenatide's insulinotropic effects by assessing (a) change from baseline to Day 4 in the postprandial blood glucose AUC, (b) change from baseline to Day 4 in fasting blood glucose, (c) change from baseline to Day 4 in average 7-point blood glucose profile, (d) change from baseline to Day 29 in HbA1c, (e) change from baseline to Day 4 in the HOMA insulin sensitivity parameters – Beta cell function, and Insulin resistance, in a pharmacodynamic safety and tolerability study in T2DM patients (study ACT6011). In this placebo controlled study, the starting dose of lixisenatide was 5 µg per s.c. injection (administered either QD or BID). The dose per injection was increased every 5th day in increments of 2.5 µg (Table 13), up to a maximum level of 20 µg during the last 4 days (Days 25 to 28) in those subjects who tolerated the dose and lixisenatide was safe. The morning injections were administered s.c. 15 min before breakfast, and the evening injections were administered 10 hours after the morning injection, 15 min before dinner.
L180: **Table 13** Planned Dosing of Lixisenatide or Placebo
L181: Planned treatment days	Dose per injection	Daily dose at QD regimen	Daily dose at BID regimen
Day 1 - Day 4	5.0 µg AVE0010	5.0 µg AVE0010	10.0 µg AVE0010
Day 5 - Day 8	7.5 µg AVE0010	7.5 µg AVE0010	15.0 µg AVE0010
Day 9 - Day 12	10.0 µg AVE0010	10.0 µg AVE0010	20.0 µg AVE0010
Day 13 - Day 16	12.5 µg AVE0010	12.5 µg AVE0010	25.0 µg AVE0010
Day 17 - Day 20	15.0 µg AVE0010	15.0 µg AVE0010	30.0 µg AVE0010
Day 21 - Day 24	17.5 µg AVE0010	17.5 µg AVE0010	35.0 µg AVE0010
Day 25 - Day 28	20.0 µg AVE0010	20.0 µg AVE0010	40.0 µg AVE0010
L182: The following PD variables were evaluated in this study:
L183: - The AUC_{[0:14h-4:55h]} for postprandial blood glucose determined for the breakfast, lunch and dinner test meal on the 4th day of each dose level;
- The AUC_{[0:14h-4:55h]} for the postprandial changes in blood glucose relative to the pre-meal value determined for the breakfast, lunch and dinner test meal at baseline (Day -1) and on the 4th day of each dose level;
- The morning fasting blood glucose levels on the 3rd and 4th day of each dose level;
- Average 7-point daily blood glucose profile: The mean of the blood glucose measurements before meals (at 0:14h, 5:14h, 10:14h after the morning injection), 90 minutes after meals (at 1:45h, 6:45h, 11:45h after the morning injection), and at bedtime (at 14:55h after the morning injection);
- Maximum postprandial blood glucose after breakfast, lunch and dinner;
- The AUC_{[0:14h-4:55h]} for the postprandial changes relative to the pre-meal value in insulin, C-peptide, and glucagon levels for the test breakfast at baseline (Day -1), on Day 4, on the 4th day of the 10 µg dose level, and on Day 28;
- HbA_{1c} measured on Day 29;
- Insulin sensitivity: The following parameters from the HOMA were analyzed:Beta cell function = [20 \times \text{fasting insulin} (\mu\text{IU/mL})] / [\text{fasting glucose} (\text{mmol/L}) - 3.5],
- Insulin resistance = [\text{fasting insulin} (\mu\text{IU/mL}) \times \text{fasting glucose} (\text{mmol/L})] / 22.5;
- Gastric emptying time (from a ^{13}\text{C}-octanoic acid breath test) for the test breakfast meal on the 4th day of the 10 µg dose level and on Day 28. The following parameters were collected:Lag phase t_{\text{lag}},
- Emptying half-life t_{1/2},
- Gastric emptying coefficient (GEC).
L184: ## Postprandial changes in blood glucose AUC relative to baseline
L185: The primary analysis variable in this trial was the area under the blood glucose concentration time curve from 0:14 h after the morning injection (administered just before standardized breakfast intake) until 4:55 h after the morning injection (AUC_{[0:14h-4:55h]}) on the 4th day of the highest individually well-tolerated dose. The change compared to baseline (Day -1) was evaluated, as summarized in Table 14 below.

L191: Significant differences in postprandial glucose were observed compared to placebo in the lixisenatide QD and BID dose groups. The mean baseline blood glucose AUC was highest in the placebo group (1007.4 h.mg/dL), which then increased slightly by 3.0 h.mg/dL by the 4th day of treatment. In contrast, in the lixisenatide groups, which had lower baseline AUC values (974.6 h.mg/dL in the QD group, and 863.6 h.mg/dL in the BID group), decreases of -378.3 and -360.0 h.mg/dL were observed, respectively, by the 4th day of treatment.
L192: There were no statistically significant differences between the lixisenatide QD and BID groups.
L193: Mean profiles of blood glucose levels across the day at baseline and at the highest well-tolerated dose are shown in Figures 6, 7 and 8 below for the 3 treatment groups. Compared to placebo, clear reductions in blood glucose levels were seen after breakfast with QD dosing of lixisenatide, and after breakfast and dinner with BID dosing of lixisenatide.

At dinner, the differences to placebo were statistically significant in the lixisenatide QD group at all dose levels apart from 5 and 10 µg.

L219: The placebo-adjusted change in the postprandial blood glucose AUC relative to the pre-meal value were significant at all dose levels for the meals at which liraglutide had been administered, i.e., at breakfast for the liraglutide QD group, and at breakfast and dinner for the liraglutide BID group.
L220: In addition, at lunch the placebo-adjusted change in the postprandial blood glucose AUC tended to increase with dose at the higher dose levels, so that the changes were statistically significant relative to the pre-meal value at dose levels of 10 \mug and above for the liraglutide QD group and at dose levels of 12.5 \mug and above for the liraglutide BID group. At dinner, the changes were statistically significant in the liraglutide QD group only at the highest dose levels of 20 \mug and the highest individually well-tolerated dose.
L221: There were no statistically significant differences in the blood glucose AUC values for the postprandial change relative to the pre-meal value between the liraglutide QD and BID groups at either of the administered doses, at breakfast or lunch. However, at dinner, differences between the liraglutide QD and BID groups were significant at each dose level, with the AUC values in the BID group being much lower than in the QD group.

L230: Numerically insulin secretion is higher for BID than placebo, but given the variability of the data, lixisenatide treatment given BID does not appear to be different from placebo.

L238: Mean baseline AUC_{[0:14h-4:55h]} values for C-peptide were lower in the lixisenatide BID group than in the placebo and lixisenatide QD groups. After breakfast on the 4th day of treatment, increased C-peptide values compared to baseline were recorded in the placebo and lixisenatide BID groups at all dose levels investigated, but in the lixisenatide QD group only at the 5 and 10 \mug dose levels. Thus only the difference between lixisenatide QD and placebo for the change from baseline in C-peptide AUC at the 20 \mug dose level was statistically significant. There were also no statistically significant differences between the 2 lixisenatide groups for the change from baseline in C-peptide AUC.
L239: Consistent with the results for insulin, the highest post-treatment changes in values were also recorded in the lixisenatide BID group. Given the variability of the data, lixisenatide treatment given BID does not appear to be different from placebo.

There were no statistically significant differences between the 2 lixisenatide groups for the change from baseline in glucagon AUC.
L248: The suppression of glucagon after the test breakfast in the lixisenatide groups is consistent with the known affects for GLP-1 agonists.

L260: ## Reduction of postprandial plasma glucose following 4 week treatment of 20\mug lixisenatide or 1.8 mg liraglutide
L261: A study was conducted to investigate the effects of repeated subcutaneous doses of 20 \mug lixisenatide as compared to 1.8 mg liraglutide in reducing postprandial plasma glucose (PPG) assessed as area under the plasma glucose concentration curve (AUC) after a standardized breakfast at the end of a 4-week treatment period in patients with type 2 diabetes.
L262: The schematic study design is shown in Figure 10:
L263: **Figure 10** Lixisenatide vs. Liraglutide study design
L264: The primary pharmacodynamic variable examined in this study was the change in \text{GLU-AUC}_{0:30-4:30} i.e., the change from baseline in the area under the plasma glucose concentration-time curve calculated using the linear trapezoidal rule, determined from glucose assessments from the time of standardized breakfast start (30 minutes after study medication injection) on Day 28 to 4 hours after injection, relative to the pre-meal glucose concentration.
L265: The change relative to baseline was determined as:
L266: \text{Change in } \text{GLU-AUC}_{0:30-4:30\text{h}} = \text{GLU-AUC}_{0:30-4:30\text{h}} \text{ on Day 28} - \text{GLU-AUC}_{0:30-4:30\text{h}} \text{ on Day -1}
L267: Secondary pharmacodynamic parameters estimated in the study were:
L268: - To assess the effects of lixisenatide as compared to liraglutide after a 4-week treatment period in patients with type 2 diabetes:on the maximum PPG excursion, and on the changes in insulin, pro-insulin, C-peptide, and glucagon plasma concentrations following a standardized breakfast
- on the 24-hour profile of plasma glucose
- on \text{HbA}_{1\text{c}}
- on satiety markers (obestatin, PYY3-36 and oxyntomodulin)
- To assess the clinical and laboratory safety profile of lixisenatide and liraglutide over a 4-week treatment period in patients with type 2 diabetes
L269: ## Plasma Glucose between 0:30-4:30h
L270: Lixisenatide reduced the corrected mean change in plasma glucose \text{AUC}_{0:30-4:30\text{h}} (\text{h}\cdot\text{mg}/\text{dL}) from baseline: -227.25 for lixisenatide compared to -72.83 for liraglutide. The estimated mean treatment difference between lixisenatide and liraglutide treatments was -154.42 [95% CI: -180.30 to -128.54] and was statistically significant (p &lt; 0.0001)
L271: ## Change from baseline in postprandial plasma glucose excursion from T0.30h to T4.30h on Day 28
L272: PPG excursion from the baseline value at Day 28 was -70.43 mg/dL in the lixisenatide group compared to -24.93 mg/dL in the liraglutide group, with a mean treatment difference estimate of -45.50 [95% CI: -55.21 to -35.80] for lixisenatide compared to liraglutide. This difference was statistically significant (p &lt; 0.0001).
L273: ## Plasma glucose concentration over 24 hours on Day 28 and Day -1
L274: Results showed that for all time points between T0.5h to T4:30h, lixisenatide reduced the increase in plasma glucose after a standardized breakfast to a greater extent than liraglutide. However, after T4h30, glucose levels were lower for liraglutide than for lixisenatide at all time points (Figure 11). This pharmacodynamic observation correlates with the pharmacokinetic characteristics of the two drugs: lixisenatide has a t_{\max} between 1 to 3.5 hours in patients with T2DM, while liraglutide has a t_{\max} between 8-12 hours1 post-dosing, therefore it is expected that lixisenatide would exert its action early compared to liraglutide.
L275: 1 Victoza product monograph, accessed at http://www.accessdata.fda.gov/drugsatfda_docs/label/2013/022341s020lbl.pdf
L276: Mean \pmSEM of raw data for 24-hour postprandial plasma glucose profiles on Day -1 and Day 28 – pharmacodynamic population
L277: Figure 11 Mean (\pmSEM) plot of 24-hour postprandial plasma glucose profiles on Day -1 and Day 28 – pharmacodynamic population and Comparative plasma profiles of lixisenatide and liraglutide.
L278: A comparison of the blood glucose lowering effect of lixisenatide and liraglutide showed that though lixisenatide lowered blood glucose to a greater extent than liraglutide between the interval of 0-5 hours (between breakfast and lunch), liraglutide had a greater glucose lowering effect compared to lixisenatide between the intervals of 5 – 16 hours post-breakfast for which comparison plots are available (Figure 12).

L286: ## 2.3.4 Is major route of elimination in humans identified?
L287: In human liver microsomes, lixisenatide showed no significant inhibition of the CYP isozymes
L288: tested (1A2, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, 3A), for which IC_{50} values were all above 20 \mu\text{mol/L} (studies F2005KIN0088, GMPK-KW 2008-0035). Lixisenatide is a peptide consisting of 44 amino acids, and is subject to standard proteolytic processes that result in degradation to small peptides and amino acids. The *in vitro* metabolism of lixisenatide in human plasma was very slow, with a mean apparent terminal elimination half-life (t_{1/2}) of approximately 35 hours. Lixisenatide was intensively metabolized in the kidney and liver S9 fractions. In humans, the percentage of lixisenatide remaining in the S9 liver and kidney fractions after 60 minutes was 8% and 11% respectively (study F2006KIN0001). Overall, 28 metabolites were detected after incubation of lixisenatide in human liver and kidney S9 fractions. All metabolites were degraded peptide products of lixisenatide. Peptides (mean molecular weight &lt;50 KD) are assumed to be eliminated through renal filtration followed by tubular reabsorption and subsequent metabolic degradation.
L289: ## 2.3.5 Does this drug prolong the QT or QTc Interval?
L290: A formal consult to review the thorough QT study was submitted to the IRT. Based on the IRT review the following observations were made:
L291: The largest upper bounds of the 2-sided 90% CI for the mean difference between lixisenatide (20 \mu\text{g} q.d. and 30 \mu\text{g} b.i.d.) and placebo were below 10 ms, the threshold for regulatory concern as described in ICH E14 guidelines. The largest lower bound of the two-sided 90% CI for the \Delta\Delta\text{QTcF} for moxifloxacin was higher than 5 ms, and the moxifloxacin profile over time is demonstrated in Figure 13, indicating that assay sensitivity was established.
L292: In this randomized, double-blind, double-dummy, multiple dose, placebo-controlled parallel study, 91 healthy subjects received lixisenatide 20 \mu\text{g} once daily, lixisenatide 30 \mu\text{g} twice daily, placebo, and a single oral dose of moxifloxacin 400 mg. Overall summary of findings is presented in Table 21.

L300: The selection of 20-µg q.d. dose as the therapeutic dose for TQT study is reasonable. The supra-therapeutic dose (30 µg b.i.d.) represents the highest dose tested. The overall exposure as well as the variability of the pharmacokinetic parameters increased largely in the antibody-positive subjects. The pharmacokinetic data in this study covered a wide range of lixisenatide exposure with mean values of C_{max} up to 209 pg/mL and AUClast up to 979 pg·h/mL in antibody-negative subjects, and C_{max} up to 945 pg/mL and AUClast up to 12500 pg·h/mL in antibody-positive subjects. The mean C_{max} and AUC of lixisenatide was approximately 40% higher at 30 µg b.i.d. than at 20 µg q.d. in antibody-negative subjects or antibody-positive subjects.
L301: The relationship between ΔΔQTcF and lixisenatide concentrations is visualized in Figure 14. Based on visual diagnostics, the exposure-response relationship can be described better with a log-linear model compared to that with a linear model. As shown in Figure 14, the slope of ΔΔQTcF versus log lixisenatide concentration is positive and significant (slope 1.5266 with p-value &lt;.0001 for a log-linear mixed effect model including intercept).
L302: However, there is no substantial QT prolongation in the exposure range at the therapeutic dose of 20 µg q.d. and the supra-therapeutic dose of 30 µg b.i.d. (Figure 14, bottom right panel).

L305: ## 2.3.6 Does the dose-response relationship for efficacy support the proposed dose and dosing regimen of 20 µg once daily?
L306: Yes, the dose-response relationship of efficacy along with the dose-response relationship of safety (discussed later) supports the proposed dosing regimen of 20 µg once daily.
L307: The sponsor studied only one dose of 20 µg QD in all the Phase 3 trials. All the trough concentrations data in the Phase 3 trials were below the limit of quantification. Thus, dose/exposure response relationship for safety and efficacy is limited to data available from a single Phase 2 trial of 13-week duration (Study #DRI6012).
L308: The selection of 20 µg QD dose in all the Phase 3 trials, was primarily based on a Phase 2 dose ranging study of 13-week treatment duration (Study #DRI6012). In this Phase 2 study, subjects were randomized to receive 5 µg, 10 µg, 20 µg, and 30 µg of lixisenatide dose as QD and BID dosing regimen. The primary efficacy endpoint for this Phase 2 study was change from baseline in HbA_{1c} at week 13. As shown in Figure 15, a clear dose response relationship was observed, where increasing doses within the QD and BID-dosing regimen resulted in increase in response in terms of HbA_{1c}. Compared to proposed 20 µg QD dosing, 10 µg BID-dosing regimen showed slight numerically greater reduction in HbA_{1c} (-0.69 with 20 µg QD vs. -0.78 with 10 µg BID-dosing). Furthermore, Figure 16 shows cumulative distribution function (CDF) plots for change from baseline HbA_{1c} between these two dosing regimens. Overlapping CDF plots between 20 µg QD and 10 µg BID dosing indicates that the distribution for change from base line in HbA_{1c} is similar across both the dosing group (Figure 16).
L309: Figure 15 Mean change in HbA_{1c} at endpoint (ITT population) (Study DRI6012)
L310: BID = twice daily; HbA_{1c} = glycosylated hemoglobin; ITT = intent-to-treat; LS = least square; n = number of patients; QD = once daily
L311: [Source: Sponsor's Summary of Clinical Efficacy report Pg 131]
L312: **Figure 16** Cumulative distribution function plot for mean change from baseline in \text{HbA}_{1\text{c}} at week 13 (Study DRI6012).
L313: AVE0010 10 µg BID = Lixisenatide 10 µg BID dosing; AVE0010 20 µg QD = Lixisenatide 20 µg QD dosing
L314: **Figure 17**, represents the dose-response relationship for secondary pharmacodynamic endpoints: A) fasting plasma glucose (FPG) and B) 2-hr post prandial glucose (PPG) at week 13. A clear dose response relationship was observed for both secondary endpoints, where increasing doses within the QD and BID-dosing regimen resulted in increase in response. Compared to proposed 20 µg QD dosing, 10 µg BID-dosing regimen showed numerically greater reduction in FPG whereas for PPG 20 µg QD dosing seems to be numerically better than 10 µg BID dosing. Lixisenatide has a short plasma half-life of around 2-4 hours. Thus, better control of FPG with BID dosing of lixisenatide is expected since BID dosing can provide glucose control at both morning and evening meal and hence that could result in better FPG with BID dosing. In addition, better effect on PPG with 20 µg QD dose is expected since with QD dosing 20 µg of dose is given at once before meal and hence more effect on PPG as compared to BID dosing where the 20 µg dose is divided into morning and evening dose of 10 µg.

L319: **Dose-Response relationship for Weight Loss:** A clear dose-response relationship for weight loss was observed for each dosing regimen, i.e., the higher the lixisenatide dose within a regimen (QD or BID) the larger the mean decrease in body weight. Figure 18 shows the mean (\pmSE) change in body weight by time and by treatment group. The data for mean change in weight (kg) from baseline to endpoint (week 13) is presented in Table 22. Compared to 10 \mug BID dose, 20 \mug QD dose have a greater weight loss change.

L325: ## 2.3.7 *Does the dose-response relationship for safety support the proposed dose and dosing regimen of 20 µg once daily?*
L326: Yes, the dose-response relationship of safety supports the proposed dosing regimen of 20 µg once daily.
L327: **Gastrointestinal Disorder:** The major safety issues associated with liraglutide were gastrointestinal disorders such as nausea and vomiting. The trough concentrations data in the Phase 3 trials were below the limit of quantification. In addition sponsor studied only one dose in all the Phase 3 trials. Thus, dose/exposure response relationship for safety is limited to data available from a single Phase 2 trial of 13-week duration (Study #DRI6012). In the Phase 2 study, a clear dose-response relationship within both regimens was seen in the number of subjects with GI related adverse events. The frequencies for GI side effects ranged from 21.8 % (5 µg) to 51.9 % (30 µg) in liraglutide QD groups and from 22.6% (5 µg) to 57.4 % (30 µg) in liraglutide BID groups. Doses higher than 20 µg in both QD and BID dosing regimen resulted in more gastrointestinal related adverse events (Table 23).

L330: **Effect on Heart Rate:** Lixisenatide was not found to affect the heart rate. No difference in heart rate was observed between 20 µg QD dose and 10 µg BID dose. Also, in through QT study no difference in heart rate was observed following 20 µg QD dose and 30 µg BID dose. Please refer to Pharmacometrics review and QT-IRT review dated 06/06/12 under IND62724 for further details.
L331: ## 2.3.8 Does the benefit risk analysis support the proposed 20 µg QD dosing over 10 µg BID dosing?
L332: Yes, the benefit risk analysis of efficacy and safety supports the proposed dosing regimen of 20 µg once daily due to the following reasons:
L333: ## **Efficacy**
L334: - In the dose ranging Phase 2 study, a clear dose response relationship was observed, where increasing doses from 5 µg to 30 µg, within the QD and BID-dosing regimen resulted in increase in response in terms of HbA1c (Figure 15).
- Compared to proposed 20 µg QD dosing, 10 µg BID-dosing regimen showed numerically slightly higher reduction in HbA1c. However, the added benefit with 10 µg BID-dosing should be weighed against narrow time window left for the administration of concomitant medication because of the effect of lixisenatide on delaying gastric emptying time. Furthermore, Figure 16 shows that cumulative distribution plots for change from baseline HbA1c is highly overlapped between the these two dosing regimens.
- Compared to 10 µg BID dose, 20 µg QD dose showed a numerically greater weight loss change.
L335: ## **Safety**
L336: - In the dose ranging Phase 2 study, doses higher than 20 µg in both QD and BID dosing regimen resulted in more gastrointestinal related adverse events (Table 23).
- Additionally, compared to 20 µg QD dosing regimen 10 µg BID dosing regimen had less gastrointestinal related side effects. However, as stated above, the added safety benefit with BID dosing regimen needs to be counterbalanced with the inconvenience of co-administration of other drugs.
L337: ## **Practicality**
L338: - Lixisenatide delays the gastric emptying time and hence concomitant medications need to be administered (b) (4) 1 hour before lixisenatide administration or with next meal for those drugs that have to be administered with food.
- Thus, with BID dosing regimen the subjects will have narrow window for taking concomitant medications and hence we agree with the sponsor's proposed 20 µg QD dosing regimen as acceptable from a practical standpoint.
L339: ## 2.3.9 Does the efficacy and safety data support the proposed one-step dosing regimen of 10 µg once daily for 14 days followed by 20 µg once daily?
L340: Yes, sponsor has proposed 1-step dosing recommendation of starting dose of 10 µg QD for 14 days followed by maintenance dose of 20 µg QD, which is acceptable from efficacy and safety standpoint.
L341: In majority of the Phase 3 trials, the sponsor used a 2-step dosing regimen. In the 2-step dosing the patients were started with the initiation dose of 10 µg QD lixisenatide for 1 week followed by 15 µg QD for 1 week, and subsequently 20 µg QD as a maintenance dose. Later in the development phase sponsor used a 1-step dosing regimen, which is also the current proposed dosing recommendation in the product label. In the 1-step dosing regimen, patients were given 10 µg dose for 2 weeks followed by 20 µg QD maintenance dose. To evaluate the impact of 1-step vs. 2-step dose increase on efficacy and safety sponsor conducted two Phase 3 trials.
L342: ## **Effect of 1-Step vs 2-Step Dose Increase on Efficacy**
L343: Sponsor's analysis of the 1-step and 2-step dose initiation in two Phase 3 studies, EFC6018 and EFC10743 is shown in Figure 19. Sponsor showed that a significant HbA1c reduction from baseline of similar magnitude with both dose increase regimens (-0.85% for the 1-step group and -0.73% for the 2-step group in Study EFC6018; -0.83% for the 2-step group and -0.92% for the 1-step group in Study EFC10743). Thus, based on sponsor's analysis, the 1-step and 2-step dose initiation regimens were similar in terms of efficacy. Such similar results were expected since the only difference between the two groups was a higher dose (15 µg compared with 10 µg) during the second week of treatment.
L344: **Study EFC10743**
L345: **Study EFC6018**
L346: **Figure 19** Time-profiles for adjusted mean change from baseline in HbA1c in Phase 3 trials EFC10743 (left) and EFC6018 (right) upon administration as placebo (red), lixisenatide 20 µg QD dose administered as one step (green), and 20 µg QD dose administered as two steps (blue)
L347: [Source: Sponsor's Summary of Clinical Efficacy report Pg 149]
L348: ## **Effect of 1-Step vs 2-Step Dose Increase on Safety**
L349: Survival plot for comparing time to GI event in two Phase 3 trials showed no difference in GI related adverse events in both the dosing regimen (Figure 20). We evaluated time to gastrointestinal adverse events in both 1-step and 2-step dosing regimen. As it is known that, the only difference in
L350: 1-step vs 2-step dosing regimen is a higher dose 15 µg (compared with 10 µg in 1-step) during the second week of treatment. After second week of the treatment, both the dose groups have same maintenance dose of 20 µg QD. Survival plot for comparing time to GI event in two Phase 3 trials showed no significant differences in GI related adverse events in both the dosing regimen (Figure 20).
L351: **Figure 20** Survival plot for time to GI event in first 30 days of the treatment in Phase 3 trials EFC10743 (left) and EFC60182 (right). Lixisenatide 20 µg QD dose administered as one step (blue), and 20 µg QD dose administered as two steps (red).
L352: ## 2.3.10 *What is the impact of Anti-drug antibody on efficacy? - Is it possible to identify the patient population early on that are likely to be non-responders?*
L353: - Higher antibody concentrations resulted in attenuated HbA1c response. Antibody concentration response analysis showed a lower change from baseline in HbA1c with increasing antibody concentrations based on week 4 and week 24 antibody concentrations.
- No correlation was observed between week 4 and week 24 antibody data. Since the week 4 antibody data is not predictive of week 24 antibody data, the analysis of antibody concentration to efficacy is exploratory in nature and cannot identify non responders early on with the drug treatment. Please see Pharmacometrics review in section 4.3 for further details.
L354: ## 2.3.11 *What are the characteristics of the exposure-calcitonin relationships (dose-response, concentration-response) with regards to thyroid safety?*
L355: Calcitonin data were collected in all Phase 3 studies that lasted over 12 weeks in duration. The sponsor implemented calcitonin monitoring via a protocol amendment when most of the Phase 3 studies were already ongoing, therefore baseline values for most of the patients were not collected.
L356: In the Phase 3 placebo-controlled studies, 33 patients (1.4%) in the lixisenatide group and 21 patients (1.6%) in the placebo group reported abnormal calcitonin on-treatment values in the range
L357: \ge 20 pg/mL to 50 pg/mL during the entire treatment period (Table 24). One lixisenatide treated patient (Patient No. 276501006 in Study EFC6015) with a maximum on-treatment calcitonin value in the range of \ge 20 pg/mL to 50 pg/mL had already reported an abnormal calcitonin value of 23.9 pg/mL at baseline, and was subsequently diagnosed with thyroid C-cell hyperplasia on Day 1. After 63 days of treatment, this patient discontinued study treatment.
L358: Ten patients (0.4%) in the lixisenatide group and 2 patients (0.1%) in the placebo group reported values \ge 50 pg/mL in Phase 3 placebo-controlled studies, but for the majority of the patients the baseline calcitonin values were not available (Table 24). One of the placebo patients with calcitonin \ge 50 pg/mL (Patient No. 276302003 in Study EFC10743) was diagnosed with medullary thyroid cancer. Furthermore, 7 (0.2%) lixisenatide patients and 3 (0.2%) placebo patients with a calcitonin on-treatment value \ge 20 pg/mL discontinued study treatment due to a treatment emergent adverse event (TEAE) of blood calcitonin increased. Lixisenatide group did not have any serious cases. One lixisenatide patient (Patient No. 642302022 in Study EFC10743) had an on-treatment potentially clinically significant abnormality (PCSA) calcitonin value who reported a serious TEAE of “blood calcitonin increased” (mild) but did not discontinue from treatment.
L359: No additional patient reported a calcitonin value \ge 50 pg/mL during the post-treatment period.

L368: the lixisenatide group (Figure 21). L373: The analysis of mean calcitonin values in the Phase 2/3 study pool showed similar results for lixisenatide versus all comparators.
L374: Calcitonin level data were also collected in the 26-week Phase 1 Study TDR11215, a study to evaluate the effect of subcutaneous doses of 20 \mug lixisenatide once daily on sperm production. The overall incidence of calcitonin levels &gt;ULN was 3.3% and 4.1% in the placebo and lixisenatide group, respectively. No subject had a calcitonin value of \ge20 pg/mL. Between baseline and the last on-treatment value, calcitonin levels remained stable over time throughout the study in both treatment groups.
L375: ## 2.4 Intrinsic Factors
L376: ## 2.4.1 What intrinsic factors (e.g., weight, gender, race, age, height, disease, genetic polymorphism, pregnancy, and organ dysfunction) influence exposure (PK usually) and/or response, and what is the impact of any differences in exposure on efficacy or safety responses?
L377: Based on population PK analysis age, gender, race and body weight have no clinically meaningful effect on pharmacokinetics of lixisenatide (See Appendix, Pharmacometrics Review)
L378: ## 2.4.1.1 Age
L379: The sponsor conducted a dedicated trial (POP11814) to assess the pharmacokinetics of lixisenatide given as a single 20 µg dose in elderly healthy subjects and matched young healthy subjects.
L380: Results from this trial showed that the mean exposure in elderly subjects was higher than in young healthy subjects (point estimates AUC_{last} 1.26 [90% CI 1.03 to 1.55] and AUC 1.29 [90% CI 1.06 to 1.57]). The t_{1/2z} was prolonged about 1.6 times in elderly subjects as compared to young subjects (point estimates t_{1/2z} 1.57 [90% CI 1.41 to 1.75]) (Table 25). C_{max} and t_{max} were comparable in both groups.
L381: **Table 25** Estimates of age ratios of lixisenatide elderly versus young subject with 90% confidence interval
L382: Parameter	Comparison	Estimate	90% CI
Cmax	Elderly/young	0.94	(0.81 to 1.09)
AUClast	Elderly/young	1.26	(1.03 to 1.55)
AUC	Elderly/young	1.29	(1.06 to 1.57)
t_{1/2z}	Elderly/young	1.57	(1.41 to 1.75)
L383: [Source: Sponsor's Table 15 Page 57, Report POP11814]
L384: As the elderly subjects generally had lower CL_{CR} rates than the young subjects (elderly: 9 out of 18 subjects had CL_{CR} values of 50 to 80 mL/min, the remaining 9 subjects had CL_{CR} values of &gt;80 mL/min; young: all 18 subjects had CL_{CR} values of &gt;80 mL/min), at least part of the increased exposure to lixisenatide in the elderly subjects may be accounted for by the decreased renal CL of lixisenatide in these subjects.
L385: In the population PK analysis, age was not identified as a significant covariate on the apparent clearance of lixisenatide. There is a trend for decrease in clearance with increasing age (Figure 22, panels A and B). Age was found to be highly correlated to cCrCL and thus after inclusion of creatinine clearance in the final model resulted in no systematic trend between inter-individual variability on clearance and age (Figure 22, panel C).
L386: Figure 22 A) Scatter plot for clearance vs. age B) Boxplot of clearance vs. age and C) Inter-individual variability on clearance vs. age
L387: ## 2.4.1.2 Gender and Race
L388: A pooled analysis of healthy subjects participating in Phase 1 studies (studies in which male and female subjects participated, and in which lixisenatide was administered at a dose of 20 µg), showed that a slightly increased exposure to lixisenatide was observed in females, which correlated well with the lower average body weight and smaller volume of distribution in these subjects (Table 26). This tendency was seen for both single and multiple dose data (Table 27). In the antibody-positive subjects, these differences by gender were confounded by the presence of immune complex (e.g., antibody) bound drug, which comprises a sizable fraction of the total circulating drug.
L389: These differences in lixisenatide exposure by gender are most likely related to differences in body weight because the population PK analysis for lixisenatide demonstrated that body weight is one of the covariates for the PK of lixisenatide; no isolated effect of gender was identified. This rationale is supported by data obtained from a Phase 1 study in healthy Chinese subjects (Table 28). In this population, the difference in
L390: body weight between males and females was much less pronounced than in the pooled Phase 1 studies, and thus the difference in exposure to lixisenatide was also much less pronounced.
L391: Any apparent difference between males and females in the exposure to lixisenatide may be due to differences in body weight between the genders rather than the differences between the genders (Table 28).
L392: In the population PK analysis of lixisenatide based upon data from studies in healthy subjects as well as patients with T2DM, no isolated effect of gender and race was identified. Figure 23 and shows that in the final model the inter-individual variability in clearance cannot be explained by gender and race, respectively.
L393: Figure 23 Inter-individual variability on clearance for A) gender B) race.

L411: ## 2.4.2 Does renal function affect Lixisenatide pharmacokinetics?
L412: The sponsor conducted a renal impairment study as a single-center, single-dose, parallel group, open-label trial investigating the pharmacokinetic profiles of lixisenatide in four groups of subjects with normal, mild, moderate and severe renal impairment. Lixisenatide was administered as a single dose of 5 µg. The dose was injected subcutaneously, into in the periumbilical abdomen of the trial subjects. The classification of renal impairment was based on creatinine clearance (glomerular filtration rate) estimated with the Cockcroft &amp; Gault formula. The study was conducted prior to the issuance of the new renal impairment guidance by FDA, and therefore used the old cut-off values. It was aimed to have eight subjects in each of the four trial groups in order to cover the complete range of creatinine clearance (Table 29).
L413: **Table 29** Classification of renal impairment groups used in Study POP6053
L414: - Renal function group I: 8 subjects with normal renal function
creatinine clearance &gt;80 mL/min, matched for age, body mass index (BMI) and gender to the renally compromised groups;
- Renal function group II: 8 subjects with mild RI
creatinine clearance 50-80 mL/min;
- Renal function group III: 8 subjects with moderate RI
creatinine clearance 30-&lt;50 mL/min;
- Renal function group IV: 8 subjects with severe RI
creatinine clearance &lt;30 mL/min, but not requiring hemodialysis and not on dialysis between 2 weeks prior to enrollment visit and end of study.
L415: [Source: report for Sponsor's Study POP6053, page 20]
L416: The sponsor was asked to re-analyze the data based on the current guidance for renal impairment (http://www.fda.gov/downloads/Drugs/GuidanceComplianceRegulatoryInformation/Guidances/UCM204959.pdf). The re-analyzed data were submitted by the sponsor on April 5, 2013. The distribution of subjects in each group changed after applying the new classification (Table 30).

L433: Overall, the average AUC0-∞ of lixisenatide was around 48 - 87% higher in the renally impaired subjects than the normal subjects. Plot of lixisenatide AUC0-last as a function of creatinine clearance is shown in Figure 24. Given the imbalance in distribution of subjects across various renal impairment categories, the
L434: distribution shows that lixisenatide exposure increases as creatinine clearance decreases, and no specific out of trend value across the various renal impairment groups was noted.
L435: The geometric mean C_{max} observed in the severe renal impairment group following a dose of 5\mu\text{g} administered via SC injection was 62.7\text{ pg/mL}. Assuming dose-linearity, a C_{max} of \sim 250\text{ pg/mL} would be expected at 20\mu\text{g}. dose. This value exceeds the mean value of 209\text{ pg/mL} seen in antibody-negative subjects at the supra-therapeutic dose of 30\mu\text{g}, as observed by the IRT team in their thorough QT study review. Even though safety information is available from only 4 patients with severe renal impairment enrolled in the Phase III study, it might be possible to dose patients with severe renal impairment at the 10\mu\text{g} dose.
L436: **Figure 24 Plot of Lixisenatide AUC_{last} as a Function of Creatinine Clearance**
L437: Box plots of C_{max}, AUC_{0-last}, and AUC are presented in Figures 25, 26 and 27, respectively. Note that the distribution of C_{max} values in patients with severe renal impairment completely overlaps with distribution of C_{max} values in patients with mild and moderate renal impairment. Similarly, the distribution of AUC_{0-t} and AUC_{0-inf} in patients with severe renal impairment also mostly overlaps with the distribution of AUC_{0-t} and AUC_{0-inf} in patients with mild and moderate renal impairment. Further note that the efficacy and safety of lixisenatide in patients with mild and moderate renal impairment have been evaluated in Phase 3 trials, which is discussed later in this section.

L444: ## **Review of Safety data from Phase 3 trials for renally impaired population:**
L445: Completed Phase 3 trials did not have any patient with severe renal impairment on lixisenatide treatment. There were approximately 30 moderate renal impairment patients on lixisenatide. A review of safety data in mild renally impaired population from completed Phase 3 trials indicated that there were signs of exaggerated nausea and vomiting and hypoglycemic events (Figure 28).
L446: **Figure 28 Safety Data in Normal and Mild Renal Impaired Patients from Completed Phase 3 Trials**
L447: Lixisenatide was also evaluated in a dedicated efficacy and cardiovascular safety trial (ELIXA) in which patients with moderate renal impairment were also enrolled. Note that in this trial, placebo unadjusted mean treatment effect is almost similar across renal function groups ranging from -0.41 to -0.52. For the placebo-adjusted treatment effect, although confidence limits overlap, mean effects increase progressively with renal impairment from normal to moderate, partly driven by differences in placebo group. The results of this trial demonstrated that reduction in HbA_{1c} for patients with moderate RI was at least similar to or numerically higher than seen in patients with normal renal function based on cross-trial comparison (Table 34 and Figure 29).

L454: ## Lixisenatide efficacy is preserved in subjects with reduced renal function
L455: **Figure 29** Change in HbA_{1c} in Subjects with Reduced Renal Function in Ongoing Cardiovascular Safety (ELIXA) Trial
L456: An examination of the safety data from the ongoing cardiovascular safety (ELIXA) trial indicated that there was a trend for higher percent of hypoglycemic events as a function of decreased renal function. A similar trend of hypoglycemic events based on renal function was also observed in the placebo group;
L457: however, compared to placebo, a slightly higher number of hypoglycemic events were observed following treatment with liraglutide (Figure 30).
L458: Figure 30 Percent Hypoglycemic Events in Ongoing Cardiovascular Safety (ELIXA) Trial
L459: A similar trend was noted for nausea and vomiting episodes, however, the incidence of nausea and vomiting was much higher following liraglutide treatment compared to placebo (Figure 31).
L460: Figure 31 Percent Nausea &amp; Vomiting Events in Ongoing Cardiovascular Safety (ELIXA) Trial
L461: ## **Reviewer's Comments:**
L462: *In subjects with mild renal impairment, C_{max} was about 66% higher and AUC was up to 48% higher in contrast to healthy subjects. No dose adjustment is recommended in these patients, however, use with caution is recommended because of exaggerated hypoglycemia, nausea and vomiting observed in Phase 3*
L463: trials.
L464: *In subjects with moderate renal impairment, C_{max} was about 35% higher and AUC was up to 65% higher in contrast to healthy subjects. There were no unique safety concerns observed in patients with moderate renal impairment from the ongoing cardiovascular safety trial. No dosing adjustment is recommended in patients with moderate RI; however, use with caution is recommended because of general concerns of dehydration leading to acute renal failures with this class of drugs. Please refer to the clinical review by Dr. Suchitra Balakrishnan for complete details of the results from efficacy and safety trial patients with moderate RI.*
L465: *In subjects with severe renal impairment, C_{max} was about 82% higher and AUC was up to 123% higher in contrast to healthy subjects. There were only 3 patients with severe RI in the efficacy and safety trial evaluating the use of lixisenatide in RI. The sponsor proposed not to use lixisenatide in patients with severe RI. However, given that efficacy of lixisenatide is preserved in patients with RI and that there is no unique safety concerns in this population, this reviewer recommends that lixisenatide should be used with caution in patients with severe RI. The dose of lixisenatide should be restricted to 10 \mug QD in patients with severe RI, and should be increased to 20 \mug QD only in patients tolerating lixisenatide who require additional glycemic control.*
L466: *The recommendation for 10 \mug QD dose in patients with severe RI is based on exposure matching. A forest plot with the recommendations is shown in Figure 32.*
L467: **Figure 32 Effect of Renal Impairment on Lixisenatide PK**
(Dashed line indicate the 80%-125% limit)
L468: ## 2.4.3 Does hepatic dysfunction affect Lixisenatide pharmacokinetics?
L469: Lixisenatide is cleared primarily by the kidney; hence hepatic dysfunction is not expected to affect the PK of lixisenatide to any relevant degree. In addition, lixisenatide exhibits only a moderate degree of protein binding (~55%) to human plasma proteins. There is therefore no relevant binding of lixisenatide to albumin, the plasma content of which is affected by hepatic impairment.
L470: The sponsor did not conduct any PK study in subjects with acute or chronic hepatic impairment. Advanced hepatic impairment was an exclusion criterion in the clinical studies.
L471: ## **Reviewer's Comments:**
L472: *No dosing adjustment is recommended in patients with hepatic impairment. This is similar to the dosing recommendation for other biologics.*
L473: ## 2.5 Extrinsic Factors
L474: ## 2.5.1 What is the effect of different injection sites on the bioavailability of Lixisenatide?
L475: The relative bioavailability of lixisenatide after SC administration in the thigh, upper arm and abdomen was assessed in a randomized, open-label, single-center, three period, cross-over trial (Study BDR6864) where single doses of lixisenatide were administered SC to 43 healthy male and female obese subjects, 18 to 65 years old, on three different occasions, each separated by a 48 hour wash-out period counting from time of dosing. The site of administration differed between the dosing days, either in the abdomen, thigh or upper arm. The dose was given as a fixed dose (10 µg) in a fixed volume (100 µl). The pharmacokinetic parameters are summarized in Table 35, and statistical analysis is presented in Table 36 below. The pharmacokinetic profile of lixisenatide is presented in Figure 33.

L490: A single SC injection of 10µg lixisenatide in the abdomen, the thigh, or the arm resulted in comparable bioavailability. Analyses of AUC_{\text{last}} and AUC showed no differences between the 3 injection sites based on the ratio estimates and the 90% CIs, which were within the equivalence range of 80% to 125%. However, the mean C_{\max} following SC injection in the thigh was slightly lower compared to the injection in the abdomen (90% CI for the ratio: [0.79, 0.94]), whereas the mean C_{\max} value for the injection in the arm was similar to that of the abdomen. However, this decrease in C_{\max} is not likely to have a clinical impact on the efficacy of lixisenatide or its safety/tolerability profile. Based on these findings lixisenatide can be administered interchangeably at these injection sites.
L491: ## 2.5.2 Drug-Drug Interactions
L492: ## 2.5.2.1 What is the CYP inhibition potential of Lixisenatide?
L493: The potential inhibitory effect of lixisenatide on the important human drug metabolizing cytochrome P450s, CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A were assessed *in vitro* in a pooled microsomal preparation from 50 human livers in study MIH0332. The model substrates used were as follows: phenacetin (CYP1A2), bupropion (CYP2B6), diclofenac (CYP2C9), S-mephentytoin (CYP2C19), dextromethorphan (CYP2D6), midazolam and testosterone (CYP3A4). The summary of estimated IC50 values for lixisenatide for human drug metabolizing cytochrome P450s is presented in Table 37 below. At the maximum tested concentration of 20 µmol/L (97 µg/mL), the observed percent inhibition ranged from 0-32%, indicating that the IC50-values for the inhibition of human CYP enzymes by lixisenatide are above 20 µmol/L. Thus, lixisenatide is not expected to cause any drug-drug interactions related to inhibition of cytochrome P450s and we agree with the sponsor's conclusions. In addition, the inhibitory effect of lixisenatide on human cytochrome P450 isozyme CYP2C8 was determined in human liver microsomes in study MIH0544.
L494: **Table 37** *In vitro* CYP enzyme inhibition potential of lixisenatide
L495: CYP Enzyme	Probe Substrate	IC50 (µmol/L)	Inhibition at 20 µmol/L AVE0010 (%)1

CYP2B6	Bupropion	> 20	9
