L499: [Source: Sponsor's report mih0544, page 15]
L500: The sponsor also examined the *in vitro* induction potential of lixisenatide for CYP1A, CYP2B6, CYP2C9 and CYP3A in primary cultures of human hepatocytes over the lixisenatide concentration range 0.037 – 37 nM (180 - 180000 pg/mL). No *in vitro* induction potential for CYP1A, CYP2B6, CYP2C9 and CYP3A was observed for lixisenatide over the concentration range 0.037 – 37 nM (180 - 180000 pg/mL) both at the enzyme activity and mRNA level.
L501: The sponsor proposed that *in vivo* drug interactions due to induction of CYP1A, CYP2B6, CYP2C9 and CYP3A by lixisenatide in plasma up to concentrations of 37 nM (180000 pg/mL) are considered unlikely. This reviewer agrees with the sponsor.
L502: ## 2.5.2.2 What is the effect of Lixisenatide co-administration on the pharmacokinetics of other drugs?
L503: Several drug interactions were evaluated by the sponsor with an objective to establish the effect of lixisenatide on gastro-intestinal motility and how it impacts the pharmacokinetic profile of other drugs, especially those sensitive to these physiological changes.
L504: ## 2.5.2.2.1 Acetaminophen
L505: This drug-drug interaction study assessed the effect of a 10 µg injection of lixisenatide on the pharmacokinetic (PK) parameters of a single dose of oral acetaminophen. This single-center, single-dose, open-label, randomized, placebo-controlled, 5-treatment, 5-period, 5-sequence crossover study was conducted in 15 healthy male and female subjects aged between 18 and 65 years, with body weight between 50 and 90 kg (males) or 40 and 80 kg (females) and a body mass index (BMI) between 18 and 35 kg/m2.
L506: A single dose (1000 mg) of acetaminophen was administered at -1 hour, +1 hour and, +4 hours relative to lixisenatide administration and -1 hour and +1 hour relative to placebo administration. There was at least a 2-day washout between each acetaminophen administration period. The treatments were administered as shown in Table 38.
L507: **Table 38** Lixisenatide and Acetaminophen Treatments and conditions of administration
L508: Treatments	Day 1
7:00 AM	8:00 AM	8.30 AM	9:00 AM	12:00 PM	1.30 PM	8.30 PM
A	AA adm.	Placebo injection	Breakfast	Lunch	Dinner
B	AA adm.	AVE0010 injection	Breakfast	Lunch	Dinner
C	AVE0010 injection	Breakfast	AA adm.	Lunch	Dinner
D	AVE0010 injection	Breakfast	AA adm.	Lunch	Dinner
E	Placebo injection	Breakfast	AA adm.	Lunch	Dinner
L509: AA adm. = acetaminophen administration
L510: [Source: sponsor's study INT6863, Table 3, Page 25]
L511: Mean (SD) acetaminophen plasma concentration-time profiles for each treatment are shown in Figure 34.
L512: **Figure 34 Mean (SD) acetaminophen plasma concentrations**
L513: [Source: sponsor's study INT6863, Figure 1, Page 65]
L514: Treatment A, where acetaminophen was given 1.5 hours before breakfast, 1 hour before placebo served as the control arm for this study. Comparison of Treatment E (acetaminophen given 30 minutes after breakfast, 1 hour after placebo) and Treatment A allowed the assessment of the effect of food on acetaminophen. When acetaminophen was administered 30 minutes following breakfast, there was a 48% reduction of C_{max} and a corresponding increase of median t_{max} from 0.25 to 2 hours.
L515: When acetaminophen was administered 1 hour before liraglutide (1.5 hours before breakfast) in Treatment B, there was no change in the rate and extent of absorption of acetaminophen compared to treatment A.
L516: When acetaminophen was administered 1 hour after liraglutide administration (30 minutes post breakfast), there was a 63% reduction in C_{max} and a corresponding shift in T_{max} from less than 1 hour to 4.5 hours. However, treatment C reflects the composite effect of food on acetaminophen, and the delaying effect of liraglutide on gastric emptying. Accounting for the effect of food on acetaminophen (treatment E), resulted in a 15% reduction of acetaminophen C_{max} due to delayed gastric emptying effect of liraglutide.
L517: Acetaminophen PK parameter ratio estimates with 90% CIs for treatment differences are reported in Table 39.

L525: E: Placebo + Acetaminophen 9 AM (T+1h)
L526: Note: Breakfast was administered at 8:30 AM, lunch at 1:30 PM, and dinner at 8:30 PM
L527: [Source: sponsor's study INT6863, Table 21, Page 71]
L528: - Statistical analysis performed by the sponsor on acetaminophen PK parameters showed that regardless of the dose-timing of lixisenatide (1 hour before and 1 hour and 4 hours after acetaminophen dosing), the AUC of acetaminophen was not affected by the administration of lixisenatide.
- Sponsor also reported that the Wilcoxon sign-rank test for the difference between treatments in t_{max} showed statistically significant results for each of the pairwise comparisons with the exception of the comparisons of treatment A versus B and D versus E.
L529: Note that the recommended maintenance dose of lixisenatide is 20 µg. Therefore, utilizing the results of this study, the sponsor tried to develop a model to support the estimation of the effect of 20 µg on the absorption of acetaminophen and also assess the effect of lixisenatide on gastric emptying of drugs with varying elimination half-lives.
L530: ## Description of model:
L531: A given dose of acetaminophen (AA_{dose}) transits from the stomach into the small intestine (AA_{gut}) from where it is absorbed into the blood stream (AA_p). From this central compartment acetaminophen is removed with the elimination rate k_{exc}. The transit of acetaminophen from the stomach into the gut, represented as k_{trans}, is inhibited by lixisenatide. This inhibitory effect of lixisenatide on this transit rate is represented as a sigmoidal Hill function. Pharmacokinetics of lixisenatide are modeled as a simple one-compartment model, where the lixisenatide dose (Lixi_{dose}) is directly absorbed into the central
L532: compartment (\text{Lixi}_p) from where it is eliminated subsequently.
L533: ## **Reviewer Comments on pharmacokinetic model:**
L534: *The main concern with this model is that it was developed based on the mean data and does not account for individual variability. In the current model, to extrapolate the effect of 20 µg dose of lixisenatide on GI emptying, sponsor assumed that the effect of 20 µg lixisenatide on k_{\text{trans}} is same as the 10 µg lixisenatide dose and made predictions for effect on k_{\text{trans}} based on mean PK profile of 20 µg dose. A better way would be to fit the model accounting for variability and develop a relationship between exposure and k_{\text{trans}}, which in turn can be used to determine the effect of 20 µg lixisenatide dose. This information has been requested from sponsor and will be documented in an addendum to this review.*
L535: *There are some other limitations as discussed below:*
L536: - *Effect of lixisenatide on drugs that have a short t_{\text{max}} similar to acetaminophen, but do not exhibit food-effect is unknown*
- *Effect of lixisenatide on drugs that are absorbed slowly (longer t_{\text{max}}), or that are intentionally formulated to be absorbed at a pH higher than the stomach (e.g. enteric coated formulations) is unknown*
- *Co-administration of lixisenatide with metformin is a very likely scenario in this population, and it is not known from this model if and to what extent lixisenatide will influence the absorption of metformin from gastro-retentive formulations; however, this co-administration may have been evaluated in Phase 3 trials*
- *It is not known how delayed gastric emptying would affect co-administered drugs which are metabolized through CYP mediated pathways.*
L537: ## **Reviewer Comments on results of the study:**
L538: *This study clearly demonstrated that a 10 µg dose of lixisenatide can have a delaying effect of up to 4 hours on absorption of drugs such as acetaminophen which are absorbed rapidly with a short t_{\text{max}} (1-3 hours). Oral medications that are dependent on threshold concentrations for efficacy, such as antibiotics, should be taken at least 1 hour before lixisenatide injection or with a meal or snack when lixisenatide is not administered.*
L539: ## 2.5.2.2.2 Warfarin
L540: This drug-drug interaction study assessed the effect of repeated subcutaneous daily doses of 20 µg lixisenatide on the pharmacokinetic (PK) and pharmacodynamic (PD) profiles of S-warfarin, in healthy male subjects.
L541: The study design was as follows:
L542: *Treatment A: Warfarin was administered on Day 1 without concomitant lixisenatide treatment.*
L543: *Treatment B: Warfarin was administered on Day 11 with concomitant lixisenatide treatment (=fourth day of 20 µg dose).*
L544: Warfarin (25 mg QD) was administered as a single dose on Day 1 of Period 1 and day 11 of Period 2. Lixisenatide was administered in Period 2 with a dose of 10 µg on days 1-7 and 20 µg QD on days 8-14. Lixisenatide was administered 30 minutes before breakfast while warfarin was administered with breakfast. The mean concentration profiles for (S)-warfarin, (R)-warfarin and lixisenatide are shown in Figure 35 below.
L545: Figure 35 Pharmacokinetic profiles of (S)-warfarin, (R)-warfarin and lixisenatide
L546: [Source: Sponsor's report int10408, Figures 6, 8, 10, pages 69, 71, 72]
L547: Statistical analysis of pharmacokinetic parameters summarized in Table 40 below for S-warfarin and Table 41 for R-warfarin showed that the 90% CIs for AUC_{last} and AUC of S-warfarin and R-warfarin were within the protocol-specified range of 80-125%, demonstrating the lack of pharmacokinetic interaction on systemic exposure. The geometric mean C_{max} of S-warfarin was 0.81, with 90% confidence intervals ranging from 0.68-0.96, and the geometric mean C_{max} of R-warfarin was 0.85, with 90% confidence intervals ranging from 0.70-1.02. However, the appearance of pharmacokinetic interaction on C_{max} could be due to a potential outlier. Excluding that subject's C_{max} value from the overall data increased the geometric mean ratio of C_{max} for S-warfarin to 0.88, and that for R-warfarin to 0.94, with a 90% confidence interval ranging from 0.82-0.95 for S-warfarin, and 0.88-0.99 for R-warfarin, indicating a lack of significant pharmacokinetic interaction also for the rate of exposure in this case. There was, however, a shift in T_{max} from 1 hour post warfarin alone treatment to 8.0 hours post warfarin+lixisenatide treatment.

L560: ## **Reviewer Comments:**
L561: *The implication of the 7-hour delay in time to peak warfarin concentration when administered 30 minutes after lixisenatide is unknown. While no dose adjustment for warfarin is required when co-administered with lixisenatide based on lack of pharmacokinetic interaction, frequent monitoring of INR in patients on warfarin and/or coumarin derivatives, as well as following of instructions in the warfarin product monograph for appropriate monitoring and dose adjustment is recommended at the time of initiation or ending of lixisenatide treatment.*
L562: ## 2.5.2.2.3 Atorvastatin
L563: This study investigated the effect of multiple doses of lixisenatide on the pharmacokinetics of multiple doses of atorvastatin given in the morning or evening. In Period 1, 40 mg atorvastatin was administered 30 minutes post-breakfast during days 1-6 for Group A, or 11.5 hrs post-breakfast during days 1-6 for group B. There was no lixisenatide treatment in Period 1. Following a 5-day washout period, in Period 2, for the AM group, lixisenatide was administered at 7:30 each morning (10 µg QD for days 1-7 followed by 20 µg QD for days 8-14) followed by breakfast at 8:00 AM and then atorvastatin at 8:30 AM. For the PM group, lixisenatide was administered at 7:30 each morning (10 µg QD for days 1-7 followed by 20 µg QD for days 8-15) followed by breakfast at 8:00 AM and then atorvastatin at 8:00 PM (2 hours post dinner).

L571: Histogram plots for the distribution of tmax differences between dosing atorvastatin with or without lixisenatide are presented in Figure 38 for the morning and evening dose of atorvastatin.
L572: **Figure 38** Histogram for the distribution of t_{max} differences between dosing atorvastatin with or without lixisenatide (morning dose- Group A; evening dose – Group B)
L573: [Source: Sponsor's report int10409, Figures 9,10 page 78]
L574: As can be seen from Table 42, there was an approximately 30% reduction in atorvastatin C_{max} when atorvastatin was administered alone in the evening compared to atorvastatin alone in the morning, and these findings are in agreement with reported values ("Plasma LIPITOR concentrations are lower (approximately 30% for C_{max} and AUC) following evening drug administration compared with morning", Lipitor label approved on 10/31/2012 sourced from http://www.accessdata.fda.gov/drugsatfda_docs/label/2012/020702s062s063lbl.pdf). The steady-state C_{max} of atorvastatin was reduced by 31%, AUC was unaltered, and t_{max} was prolonged (with change in t_{max} ranging from -1 to 9 hours) when atorvastatin was administered in morning 1 hour after lixisenatide injection. However, when atorvastatin was administered in evening 12.5 hours following lixisenatide administration in the morning, C_{max} of atorvastatin was increased by 66%, AUC was increased by 27%, and t_{max} was unaltered (with change in t_{max} ranging from -3 to 2 hours).
L575: There was no explanation for the increased atorvastatin exposure observed for the evening dose of atorvastatin together with lixisenatide compared to atorvastatin alone. Atorvastatin t_{max} for the evening dose was not affected, hence it is likely that lixisenatide induced effect on delayed gastric emptying did not last for 12.5 hours. Comparison of C_{max} and AUC values for atorvastatin when it was administered in the evening following lixisenatide morning administration, to atorvastatin administered alone in the morning showed a 25% increase in C_{max} and 18% increase in AUC for the evening administration.
L576: Point estimates for the 2 main metabolites of atorvastatin indicated an increase of C_{max} and AUC in combination treatment with lixisenatide compared to atorvastatin alone. The increase was similar in the evening and morning dose groups, except for ortho-hydroxy-atorvastatin C_{max} in the morning dose group. The parallel increase of exposure for the metabolites and parent compound suggested that lixisenatide did not affect atorvastatin metabolism.

L580: [Source: Sponsor's report int10409, Tables 17-19, 22, 23, 26, 27 pages 76-82]
L581: Prolongation in t_{max} by up to 9 hours following AM dosing is not likely to affect the steady-state
L582: concentrations of atorvastatin, but it may affect the time it takes to reach steady-state. For a chronically administered drug such as atorvastatin, total exposure at steady-state (AUC_T) is the most important pharmacokinetic parameter. There is an 18% increase in AUC following evening administration of atorvastatin following lixisenatide morning administration, compared to atorvastatin administered alone in the morning. This increase in total exposure may not be clinically significant. In addition, safety of atorvastatin when given with lixisenatide is also addressed based on data from phase 3 trials. Based on a survey of common cardiovascular concomitant medications used in the completed Phase 3 placebo-controlled studies, sponsor reported that statins and statin combinations represented 38% of the medications. Overall, in the Phase 3 placebo-controlled studies, 1717 patients (lixisenatide: 1087/2869, 37.9%; placebo: 630/1639, 38.4%) were enrolled, who reported concomitant statin or statin combination use (source: *Integrated Summary of Safety, Table 26, page 109*). Patients with concomitant use of statins (or statin combinations) reported more treatment-emergent adverse events during the entire treatment period than patients with no concomitant statin use. However, this trend was observed in both the lixisenatide and placebo groups (85.3% in the lixisenatide group and 79.0% in the placebo group with statin use compared with 76.7% in the lixisenatide group and 65.2% in the placebo group without statin use) (source: *Integrated Summary of Safety, Appendix 1.2.2.12, Page 1062*). Neither myopathy nor increase in creatinine phosphokinase was reported in the Phase 3 trials.
L583: ## **Reviewer Comments:**
L584: *Based on safety information from Phase 3 trials, and information garnered from approved label for atorvastatin that LDL-C reduction is the same regardless of the time of day of drug administration, no dose adjustment is recommended for atorvastatin. However, because of prolongation in time it may take to reach steady-state for atorvastatin, patients taking atorvastatin should be advised to take atorvastatin preferably 1 hour before lixisenatide administration.*
L585: ## 2.5.2.2.4 Ramipril
L586: This study assessed the effect of repeated subcutaneous doses of 20 µg lixisenatide on the pharmacokinetic profile of ramipril and its active metabolite ramiprilat, and the pharmacodynamic response to ramipril in 30 healthy male and female subjects between 18 and 45 years of age.
L587: This was a single-center, open-label, randomized, 2-treatment, 2-period, 2-sequence crossover study with a minimum washout period of 23 days (from last ramipril dosing day of the first period to first ramipril dosing day of the 2nd period). In treatment A, subjects received a daily dose of ramipril 5 mg for 6 days (Day 1 to Day 6). In treatment B, subjects received a run-in 10 µg daily dose of lixisenatide for 7 days (Day 1 to Day 7) followed by a 20-µg daily dose of lixisenatide for 7 days (Day 8 to Day 14) administered concomitantly with 6 daily doses of 5 mg ramipril on Day 9 to Day 14.
L588: Mean plasma ramipril, ramiprilat and lixisenatide concentration-time profiles and ramiprilat trough concentrations separated by treatment are shown in Figure 39.
L589: Figure 39 Mean plasma ramipril, ramiprilat and lixisenatide concentration-time profiles and ramiprilat trough concentrations
L590: [Source: sponsor's study INT10782, Figures 2, 4, 6, 7, Pages 66-69]
L591: The statistical analysis of pharmacokinetic parameters is summarized in the Table 44 below.
L592: Table 44 Treatment ratio estimates with 90% confidence intervals for ramipril and ramiprilat
L593: Ramipril
Parameter	Comparison	Estimate	90% CI
AUC0-24h(ng.h/mL)	Ramipril + lixisenatide vs. Ramipril	1.21	(1.06 to 1.39)
Cmax (ng/mL)	Ramipril + lixisenatide vs. Ramipril	0.37	(0.29 to 0.46)
Ramiprilat
Parameter	Comparison	Estimate	90% CI
AUC0-24h(ng.h/mL)	Ramipril + lixisenatide vs. Ramipril	1.11	(1.06 to 1.16)
Cmax (ng/mL)	Ramipril + lixisenatide vs. Ramipril	1.02	(0.92 to 1.14)
L594: [Source: sponsor's study INT10782, Tables 22, 24, Pages 73, 76]
L595: When ramipril was administered with breakfast 30 minutes post lixisenatide injection, the steady-state C_{max} of ramipril was reduced by 73% compared to ramipril administered alone. Steady-state AUC for ramipril was increased by 21%. T_{max} was prolonged from 0.5 hours with ramipril alone to 2.8 hours
L596: following ramipril given 30 minutes post lixisenatide injection. For ramipril, the 90% CI for the ratio estimates (1.06-1.39) for AUC_{0-24} (equal to AUC_t) was outside the upper bound of the reference interval of 0.80 – 1.25. For C_{max}, the 90% confidence intervals (0.29-0.46) were completely outside this range. Therefore a lack of interaction between ramipril and lixisenatide cannot be concluded.
L597: For ramiprilat, the active moiety of ramipril, however, there was essentially no change in C_{max} (ratio 1.02) and AUC (ratio 1.11) between ramipril+lixisenatide and ramipril treatments. T_{max} for ramiprilat, however, was prolonged from 2.5 hours with ramipril alone to 5.0 hours following ramipril given 30 minutes post lixisenatide injection. Mean trough concentrations of ramiprilat were similar between the two treatments (1.85 ng/mL following ramipril alone vs. 1.94 ng/mL following ramipril+lixisenatide). The 90% CIs for the ratio estimates of lixisenatide + ramipril versus ramipril alone were entirely within the [0.80 – 1.25] boundaries for C_{max} and AUC_{0-24h} (equal to AUC_t) of ramipril's active metabolite ramiprilat, indicating a lack of interaction of repeated doses of lixisenatide at a dose of 20 µg on the repeated-dose pharmacokinetics of ramiprilat after a 5-mg dose of ramipril at steady state.
L598: ## **Reviewer Comments:**
L599: *Ramipril is almost completely metabolized in the body to ramiprilat, which has about 6 times the ACE inhibitory activity of ramipril. Since the total exposure and trough concentrations of ramiprilat did not change following administration with lixisenatide compared to lixisenatide alone, no dose adjustment for ramipril is suggested when co administered with lixisenatide.*
L600: ## 2.5.2.2.5 *Digoxin*
L601: This study assessed the effect of repeated subcutaneous (SC) doses of 20 µg lixisenatide on the pharmacokinetic (PK) profile of digoxin in 24 healthy male and female subjects between 18 and 45 years of age inclusive.
L602: This was a single-center, open-label, randomized, 2-sequence, 2-period, 2-treatment crossover study with a minimum 14 days washout period between the 2 administration periods.
L603: There were 2 treatment arms in the study. In Treatment A, digoxin 0.25 mg BID was administered on Day 1 followed by 0.25 mg QD for 6 days (Days 2-7). In Treatment B, lixisenatide was dosed with a starting dose of 10 µg QD for 7 days (Day 1 to Day 7) followed by an up titration to 20 µg QD for 10 days (Day 8 to Day 17). Concomitantly to lixisenatide, digoxin was administered 0.25 mg BID for 1 day (in the morning and evening of Day 11), followed by digoxin 0.25 mg QD for 6 days (in the morning of Days 12-17). The wash-out between the 2 treatment periods was at least 14 days.
L604: Mean plasma digoxin concentration-time profiles separately by treatment are shown in Figure 40.
L605: **Figure 40 Mean (+SD) digoxin plasma concentrations**
L606: [Source: sponsor's study INT10783, Figure 2, Page 68]
L607: Statistical analysis of digoxin pharmacokinetic parameters is summarized in the Table 45 below.
L608: **Table 45 Treatment ratio estimates of digoxin plus lixisenatide versus digoxin alone with 90% CI**
L609: Parameter	Group / Comparison	Estimate	90% CI
C_{\text{max}} (ng/mL)	Digoxin+Lixisenatide vs Digoxin	0.74	(0.64 to 0.86)
AUC_{\tau} (ng.h/mL)	Digoxin+Lixisenatide vs Digoxin	0.94	(0.87 to 1.01)
L610: [Source: sponsor's study INT10783, Table 22, Page 73]
L611: When digoxin was administered with breakfast 30 minutes post lixisenatide injection, the steady-state C_{\text{max}} of digoxin was reduced by 26% compared to digoxin administered alone. Steady-state AUC for digoxin remained unchanged between the treatments. T_{\text{max}} was prolonged from 0.5 hours with digoxin alone to 2 hours following digoxin given 30 minutes post lixisenatide injection.
L612: The 90% CI for AUC_{\tau} of digoxin was entirely within the protocol-specified boundaries [80, 125%], following repeated doses of 20 µg lixisenatide on repeated dose of digoxin 0.25 mg, indicating that lixisenatide did not affect the overall exposure of warfarin when administered concomitantly.
L613: The lower bound for the 90% CI for digoxin C_{\text{max}} was however, outside the protocol-specified boundaries (geometric mean of the ratio estimate of digoxin plus lixisenatide versus digoxin alone 0.74, with 90% CI of 0.64-0.86), demonstrating an influence of lixisenatide on the rate of digoxin exposure.
L614: The shift of median T_{\text{max}} values for digoxin from 0.5 hour when digoxin was administered alone to 2 hours when administered concomitantly with lixisenatide was possibly due to the known delaying effect of lixisenatide on gastric emptying.
L615: ## **Reviewer Comments:**
L616: *Due to the lixisenatide induced delay in time to peak digoxin concentrations, careful monitoring of digoxin levels are recommended. The health care provider should be cautioned to follow instructions in the product monograph for digoxin for appropriate dose adjustment, if necessary when administering with lixisenatide.*
L617: ## 2.5.2.2.6 Oral Contraceptives
L618: This study assessed the effect of a 10 µg injection of lixisenatide on the pharmacokinetic parameters of a single dose of oral contraceptive (OC) at various time points in relation to a 10 µg subcutaneous (SC) injection of lixisenatide in healthy post-menopausal female subjects.
L619: This was a single-center, single-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized, 5-treatment, 5-period crossover study in 25 healthy post-menopausal female subjects, aged between 40 and 65 years, with body weight between 50 and 80 kg and a body mass index (BMI) between 18 and 28 kg/m2, and not using hormonal therapy.
L620: The subjects received 10 µg (0.1 mL) lixisenatide once daily (QD) on Day 1 and/or Day 2, depending on treatment regimen via SC administration at 8 PM on Day 1 and at 8 AM on Day 2 with concomitant treatment of 1 tablet of an oral contraceptive (OC) (Minidril, containing 0.03 mg ethinylestradiol and 0.15 mg levonorgestrel). The reference therapy was a matching placebo administered SC at 8 PM on Day 1 and at 8 AM on Day 2 with concomitant treatment 1 tablet of OC. Five different treatment plans were conducted as shown in Table 46 below. The single dose OC was administered at -1 hour relative to placebo injection (treatment A) on Day 2, -1 hour relative to lixisenatide injection (Treatment C) on Day 2, + 1 hour relative to lixisenatide injection (treatment D) on Day 2, + 4 hours (treatment E) relative to lixisenatide injection on Day 2 and + 11 hours (treatment B) relative to lixisenatide injection on Day 1.

L634: [Source: sponsor's study INT6052, Table 27, Page 72]
L635: Statistical analysis performed on ethinylestradiol pharmacokinetic parameters showed the following:
L636: - C_{\max} of ethinylestradiol reduced 52% when oral contraceptive was administered 1 hour (treatment D) compared to placebo (treatment A). When the OC was administered 4 hours (treatment E) after lixisenatide injections, the reduction in ethinylestradiol C_{\max} was 39% compared to OC administration with placebo injection (treatment A). The ratio estimate values
L637: were 0.48 (D versus A) and 0.61 (E versus A) with 90% CIs outside the limits [0.80; 1.25] (Table 47).
L638: - C_{\max} of ethinylestradiol when oral contraceptive was administered 11 hours after (treatment B) or 1 hour before lixisenatide injection (treatment C) was similar to that observed after oral contraceptive with placebo injection (treatment A). The ratio estimates were 0.99 (B versus A) and 0.93 (C versus A) with 90% CI interval within the limits [0.80; 1.25] indicating that lixisenatide injection does not change C_{\max} of ethinylestradiol when oral contraceptive is administered 11 hours after (treatment B) or 1 hour before lixisenatide injection (treatment C) (Table 47).
- AUC_{\text{last}} and AUC of ethinylestradiol after co-administration of oral contraceptive with lixisenatide, independent of the relative time point between both medications, were similar to those observed after administration of oral contraceptive with placebo. The ratio estimate values were between 0.95 and 1.01 for AUC_{\text{last}} and 0.96 and 1.06 for AUC. Their 90% CIs were within the interval [0.80; 1.25] indicating that lixisenatide injection does not affect AUC_{\text{last}} and AUC of ethinylestradiol (Table 47).
- The median t_{\max} of ethinylestradiol was increased from 2 hours to 4 or 3 hours when oral contraceptive was administered 1 hour (treatment D) or 4 hours (treatment E) after lixisenatide injection compared to oral contraceptive with placebo injection (treatment A). However, no effect on the median t_{\max} was observed when oral contraceptive was administered 1 hour before (treatment C) or 11 hours (treatment B) after lixisenatide injection.
L639: Similarly, statistical analysis performed on levonorgestrel pharmacokinetic parameters showed the following:
L640: - C_{\max} of levonorgestrel reduced by 46% when oral contraceptive was administered 1 hour (treatment D) after lixisenatide injection. When the OC was administered 4 hours (treatment E) after lixisenatide injection, the reduction in C_{\max} of levonorgestrel was 20% compared to OC administration with a placebo injection (treatment A). The ratio estimate values were 0.54 (D versus A) and 0.80 (E versus A) with lower limits of 90% CIs (0.48 and 0.70, respectively) smaller than 0.8 indicating a decrease in C_{\max} of levonorgestrel when oral contraceptive was administered 1 hour (treatment D) or 4 hours (treatment E) after lixisenatide injection (Table 47).
- C_{\max} of levonorgestrel when oral contraceptive was administered 11 hours after (treatment B) or 1 hour before lixisenatide injection (treatment C) was similar to that observed after oral contraceptive with placebo injection (treatment A). The ratio estimates were 1.00 (B versus A) and 1.01 (C versus A) with 90% CI interval within the limits [0.80; 1.25] indicating that lixisenatide injection does not change C_{\max} of ethinylestradiol when oral contraceptive is administered 11 hours after (treatment B) or 1 hour before lixisenatide injection (treatment C) (Table 47).
- AUC_{\text{last}} and AUC of levonorgestrel after co-administration of oral contraceptive with lixisenatide, independent of the relative time point between both medications, were similar to those observed after administration of oral contraceptive with placebo. The ratio estimate values were between 1.00 and 1.09 for AUC_{\text{last}} and 0.99 and 1.20 for AUC. Their lower limits of 90% CIs were greater than 0.8 indicating that lixisenatide injection does not affect AUC_{\text{last}} and AUC of ethinylestradiol (Table 47).
- The median t_{\max} of levonorgestrel was increased from 1 hour to 4 or 2 hours when oral contraceptive was administered 1 hour (treatment D) or 4 hours (treatment E) after lixisenatide injection compared to oral contraceptive with placebo injection (treatment A). However, no effect on the median t_{\max} was observed when oral contraceptive was administered 1 hour before (treatment C) or 11 hours after lixisenatide injection (treatment B).
L641: ## Reviewer Comments:
L642: *The clinical implications of reduced peak exposure of ethinylestradiol and levonorgestrel when the oral contraceptive was administered 1 hour or 4 hours after lixisenatide injection, is unknown. It is quite possible that oral contraceptives depend on threshold concentrations for efficacy. Therefore, patients should be advised to take oral contraceptives at least 1 hour before lixisenatide administration or 11 hours after the morning dose of lixisenatide.*
L643: ## 2.6 General Biopharmaceutics
L644: ## 2.6.1 *What is absolute bioavailability and disposition of Lixisenatide?*
L645: The sponsor reported that one relative bioavailability study in non-diabetic subjects (study BDR12546) included an IV treatment arm to assess the absolute bioavailability of lixisenatide. They report that the absolute bioavailability was determined for the SC administration using the reference formulation with a lixisenatide concentration of 100 µg/mL, which was also used for the IV injection. The absolute bioavailability was reported to be 32%, for both AUClast and AUC, with a 90% CI range of 24% to 42%. However, the sponsor only provided a protocol for this study, and in absence of a full study report, these reported results cannot be verified.
L646: ## 2.6.2 *Is bioequivalence established between the to-be-marketed formulation and the Phase 3 trial formulation and how does it relate to the overall product development?*
L647: Proposed commercial formulation is the same as the one used in Phase I, II and III studies. An overview on the formulation history is shown in Figure 42.
L648: **Figure 42** Formulation history of lixisenatide parenteral formulations
L649: [Source: sponsor's Quality overall summary, module 2.3.P, Figure 2, Page 10]
L650: ## 2.6.3 Are the two to-be-marketed strengths bioequivalent?
L651: The sponsor conducted a study to determine the bioequivalence of 2 different formulations with different lixisenatide concentrations (50 µg vs. 100 µg lixisenatide/ mL of lixisenatide) in 90 healthy male and female subjects, aged 18 to 45 years.
L652: The test product, lixisenatide 50 µg/mL solution for injection was available in a 3-mL (b) (4) cartridge. A dose of 10 µg (0.2 mL of a 50 µg/mL solution) was used in the study. The reference product, lixisenatide 100 µg/mL solution for injection was also available in a 3-mL (b) (4) cartridge. A dose of 10 µg (0.1 mL of 100 µg/mL solution) was used in the study.
L653: The study design was a single-center, open-label, randomized, single-dose, 3-sequence, 3-period replicate, 2-treatment crossover study. Healthy subjects, stratified by sex, were randomly allocated to 1 of 3 treatment sequences on Day 1 of Period 1 to receive a single dose of 10 µg lixisenatide in 1 of 2
L654: concentrations, 50 µg/mL or 100 µg/mL, in each period (Table 48), with 2 replications of the 100 µg/mL formulation (reference). Doses were separated by a washout period of 48 to 96 hours.
L655: **Table 48** Treatment sequences for the bioequivalence study
L656: Sequence	Treatment Period
1	2	3
1	Reference	Reference	Test
2	Test	Reference	Reference
3	Reference	Test	Reference
L657: Test = test formulation = 10 µg lixisenatide at a concentration of 50 µg/mL
L658: Reference = Reference formulation = 10 µg lixisenatide at a concentration of 100 µg/mL
L659: [Source: sponsor's study BEQ11094, Table 1, Page 16]
L660: Mean (SD) plasma lixisenatide concentration-time profiles for the test and the 2 reference treatments are shown in Figure 43.
L661: **Figure 43** Mean (+ SD) lixisenatide plasma concentrations
L662: [Source: sponsor's study BEQ11094, Figure 1, Page 54]
L663: Lixisenatide pharmacokinetic parameter ratio estimates with 90% CIs for treatment differences are reported in Table 49.
L664: **Table 49** Relative bioavailability analysis of lixisenatide test formulation versus lixisenatide reference formulation with 90% confidence interval
L665: Parameter	Comparison	Estimate	90% CI
Cmax	Test vs. Reference	0.89	(0.84 to 0.94)
AUClast	Test vs. Reference	0.83	(0.78 to 0.89)
AUC	Test vs. Reference	0.86	(0.81 to 0.92)
L666: AUC values with more than 20% extrapolation rate were not taken into account.
L667: [Source: sponsor's study BEQ11094, Table 17, Page 59]
L668: The 90% CI for Cmax and AUC of lixisenatide were within the protocol-specified boundaries [80, 125%], demonstrating bioequivalence for these parameters. However, for the parameter AUClast, the lower bound of the 90% CI (0.78-0.89) was outside the protocol-specified boundaries [80, 125%]. The geometric mean of the ratio estimate was 0.83.
L669: The within-subject variability was about 27% for AUC and AUClast and 22% for Cmax; since the estimated within-subject standard deviation for the reference formulation (\sigma_{wR}) was &lt; 0.30 for any log-transformed parameter, the scaled average bioequivalence (SABE) analysis described in Haidar2 was not applicable. Hence, the sponsor conducted the standard average bioequivalence (ABE) analysis.
L670: ## **Reviewer Comment:**
L671: *Based on the bioequivalence for the parameters AUC and Cmax, the sponsor demonstrated that the 100 µg/mL and the 50 µg/mL formulations of lixisenatide were bioequivalent. For AUClast the point estimate was within the bioequivalence interval but the lower limit of the confidence interval fell just below the bioequivalence limit.*

L685: ## 2.8 Addendum
L686: Information from four (4) new clinical pharmacology studies were available in the re-submission of Lixisenatide NDA (208471).
L687: Bioavailability of a single 20 µg dose of lixisenatide from solutions of 200 µg/mL or 400 µg/mL compared with 100 µg/mL, and the absolute bioavailability of a 5 µg dose of lixisenatide from a 100 µg/mL solution was evaluated in study BDR12546.
L688: The dose-response relationship of different doses of lixisenatide (2.5, 5, 10, and 20 µg) on glucose profiles and gastric emptying in healthy subjects was evaluated in study PDY12545.
L689: The effect of lixisenatide doses of 5 µg and 10 µg on postprandial plasma glucose in pediatric (10 - 17 years old) and adult patients with type 2 diabetes was evaluated in study PKD11475.
L690: The effect of repeated doses of lixisenatide 20 µg QD on postprandial plasma glucose compared to liraglutide 1.2 mg QD or 1.8 mg QD was evaluated in study PDY12625.
L691: Findings from these 4 studies are discussed in this addendum.
L692: ## 2.8.1 Absolute and Relative Bioavailability of Lixisenatide (Study BDR12546)
L693: The relative bioavailability of a subcutaneous (SC) injection of 20 µg lixisenatide using concentrations of 200 and 400 µg/mL compared with 100 µg/mL was evaluated in study BDR12546.
L694: In this study, Lixisenatide solutions for injection were supplied in 3 dosage strengths: 100, 200, and 400 µg/mL. The doses evaluated were 20 µg for subcutaneous injection and 5 µg for IV infusion. The reference treatment (A) was a 200 µL subcutaneous injection of a 100 µg/mL solution for injection. The two test treatments were: treatment B, available as a 100 µL subcutaneous injection of a 200 µg/mL solution for injection (Test 1), and treatment C, available as a 50 µL subcutaneous injection of a 400 µg/mL solution for injection (Test 2). The intravenous administration (treatment D) was a 30 seconds IV infusion of 50 µL of the 100 µg/mL solution for injection.
L695: Plasma lixisenatide concentration time profiles following the four treatments are shown in Figure 45 below:
L696: **Figure 45: Mean (+SD) lixisenatide plasma concentrations after IV infusion and SC dosing**
L697: Source eCTD module 5.3.1.2, Study BDR12546, Figure 6, page 64 and Figure 8 page 65.
L698: The absolute bioavailability of the SC administration of the 100 µg/mL formulation was 32% for total lixisenatide.
L699: As the lixisenatide concentration in the formulation increased (and thus decreasing volume of injection), the relative bioavailability for AUC_{last} and AUC also increased. Compared to treatment A (20 µg, 200 µL of 100 µg/mL solution) there was an approximate 30% increase in the AUC values for lixisenatide following treatment B (20 µg, 100 µL of 200 µg/mL). Compared to treatment A (20 µg, 200 µL of 100 µg/mL solution) there was an approximate 45-50% increase in the AUC values for lixisenatide following treatment C (20 µg, 50 µL of 400 µg/mL) (Table 51). The 90% CI for these comparisons were outside the bioequivalence range. For the parameter C_{max}, however, the 2 treatments were still within the bioequivalence-range of 80% to 125% (Table 52).

L714: ## 2.8.2 Dose-response relationship of different doses of liraglutide on glucose profiles and gastric emptying (Study PDY12545)
L715: The dose-response relationship of different doses of liraglutide on postprandial plasma glucose following standardized liquid meal challenge compared to placebo was evaluated in study PDY12545. This study also evaluated the dose-response relationship of different doses of liraglutide on postprandial insulin concentration compared to placebo, and the effect of different doses of liraglutide on C-peptide, glucagon profiles and gastric emptying.
L716: There was dose dependent decrease in fasting plasma glucose (FPG) following liraglutide compared to placebo prior to the administration of the standardized liquid meal. Following administration of the meal, a dose-dependent reduction in PPG was demonstrated from the 2.5 µg dose up to the 20 µg dose compared to placebo. However, the effect appears to plateau around the 10 µg dose, as there was no substantial difference between the 10 and 20 µg doses (Figure 46). Within a period of 3 hours after meal challenge, the marked inhibitory effect on gastric emptying diminished at all dose levels, indicating that the major driver for the reduction of PPG excursion was the glucose independent slowing of gastric emptying.
L717: **Figure 46: Plasma glucose (mmol/L) data from T-1H30 to T5H – Mean (+/- SEM)**
L718: Source eCTD module 5.3.4.1, Study PDY12545, Figure 3, page 56.
L719: The mean differences between the 4 liraglutide dose levels and placebo and the corresponding 90% CIs for the primary and secondary endpoints are provided in Table 53.

L722: For pharmacodynamic assessments, T0 reference is the meal challenge timepoint (i.e. 1 hour after lixisenatide administration).
L723: Source eCTD module 5.3.4.1, Study PDY12545, Table 13, pages63-64.
L724: An evaluation of post-prandial glucose showed that there was a dose-dependent reduction in PPG-Cmax across the 2.5 µg to 10 µg lixisenatide doses compared to placebo. However, though mean PPG-Cmax was higher for the 20 µg dose than the 10 µg dose, it was lower than the mean PPG-Cmax for the 2.5 and 5 µg doses.
L725: One hour after dosing of lixisenatide, a liquid meal was administered together with 1000 mg acetaminophen. The appearance of acetaminophen in plasma was reduced and delayed compared to placebo depending on the dose of lixisenatide administered, as acetaminophen was used as a probe for gastric emptying, which was delayed by lixisenatide in a dose-dependent manner.
L726: **Figure 47: Mean (\pm SEM) Insulin (pmol/L) and C-peptide (pmol/L) data from T-1H30 to T5H**
L727: Source eCTD module 5.3.4.1, Study PDY12545, Figure 10, page 73 and Figure 1, page 84.
L728: As a consequence of the reduction in FPG, small and transient increases in insulin concentration were observed prior to the meal challenge following lixisenatide injection. Following the standardized liquid meal and corresponding to the reduction of PPG excursion, insulin secretion was reduced in a dose-
L729: dependent manner up to 3 hours post meal challenge, supporting the delay in gastric emptying as the mode of action in this study (Figure 47). The 20 µg lixisenatide dose appeared to produce a more sustained delay in insulin secretion than the 10 µg dose.
L730: Similar observations were also seen for C-peptide (Figure 47).
L731: The exposure to lixisenatide increased with the dose injected, but over the dose range of 2.5 µg to 20 µg, exposure increased in a slightly less than dose-proportional manner.
L732: The exposure-effect relationships (PK/PD) for the different PD parameters, showed that, in general, a plateau value representing the maximum effect in this study was reached with lixisenatide 20 µg, that did not differ greatly from that achieved with lixisenatide 10 µg. The results achieved with lixisenatide 5 µg tended to overlap with the 2 higher doses. The effect of the lixisenatide 2.5 µg dose produced only a marginal effect compared to placebo.
L733: The influence of lixisenatide on the appearance of acetaminophen in plasma as a probe to show the extent of a delay of gastric emptying showed that the effect of the lixisenatide 10 µg dose did not completely reach the plateau, and the effect of the lixisenatide 2.5 µg dose over the 3 hours following dosing with acetaminophen was similar to that for placebo.
L734: *The results of the study reasonably support sponsor's interpretation of the data. However, the gastric emptying effects of lixisenatide have been reviewed in the initial submission of the NDA, and the findings of this study do not change our recommendation regarding co-administration of lixisenatide with other drugs.*
L735: ## 2.8.3 Effect of lixisenatide on postprandial plasma glucose in pediatric (10 - 17 years old) and adult patients with type 2 diabetes (Study PKD11475)
L736: The effects of a single subcutaneous lixisenatide dose of 5 µg and 10 µg as compared to placebo in reducing postprandial plasma glucose (PPG) assessed as area under the plasma glucose concentration curve after a standardized liquid meal (breakfast) in type 2 diabetic pediatric population (10-17 years old) and adults as controls was evaluated in study PKD11475.
L737: The population to be studied comprised patients with T2DM, with or without a stable background metformin therapy, on a regimen of diet and exercise with an age ranging from 10 years to less than 18 years for the pediatric population and 18 to 65 years for the adult population.
L738: In the pediatric evaluable PD population, the corrected plasma glucose-AUC0:30h-4:30h was decreased by single doses of lixisenatide 5 and 10 µg compared to placebo, but the differences versus placebo were not statistically significant (Table 54). In addition, there was no statistically significant difference between lixisenatide 10 and 5 µg.

L761: ## 2.8.4 Effect of repeated doses of lixisenatide 20 µg QD on postprandial plasma glucose compared to liraglutide 1.2 mg QD or 1.8 mg QD (Study PDY12625)
L762: The effects of repeated subcutaneous doses of lixisenatide 20 µg once daily (QD) as compared to liraglutide 1.2 mg QD or 1.8 mg QD in reducing postprandial plasma glucose assessed as area under the plasma glucose-concentration-time curve after a standardized breakfast at the end of an 8-week treatment period in patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM) not adequately controlled with insulin glargine (with or without metformin) was investigated in study PDY12625.
L763: Male and female patients, 18 to 75 years, inclusive, with T2DM, diagnosed at least 1 year at the time of the screening visit, not adequately controlled with insulin glargine alone or combined with or NPH alone or combined with a stable dose of metformin with or without a dipeptidyl peptidase-4 (DPP-4) inhibitor or a sulfonylurea were enrolled in this study.
L764: The mean 24-hour plasma glucose profiles, based on 17-time point assessments, were similar across the 3 treatment groups at baseline (Day -3). On Day 56 (Week 8), greatest reductions with lixisenatide were seen postbreakfast (only up to 4 hours and 30 minutes after injection of lixisenatide); after this period, plasma glucose levels were similar to baseline values (Figure 50). Treatment with liraglutide (1.2 and 1.8 mg), however, resulted in consistent glucose reductions throughout the day.
L765: **Figure 50:** Mean (\pm SE) concentrations for 24-hour plasma glucose profiles (mmol/L) on Day -3 and Day 56 - PD population
L766: Source eCTD module 5.3.4.2, Study PDY12625, Figure 5, page 105.
L767: The primary endpoint of this study was a comparison of the performance of lixisenatide against liraglutide in reducing PPG AUC between 0:30 and 4:30 h after a standardized solid breakfast. Lixisenatide demonstrated a greater effect than liraglutide (1.2 and 1.8 mg) in reducing PPG AUC0:30–4:30h after a standardized solid breakfast, however, liraglutide exhibited a sustained plasma glucose decrease at
L768: lunch and dinner times. The greater decrease in plasma glucose after breakfast in lixisenatide-treated patients can probably be related to the significant delayed gastric emptying following lixisenatide compared to liraglutide.
L769: The profiles of corrected C-peptide concentrations from the start of standardized solid breakfast (T0H30 post lixisenatide injection) up to 5 hours later (T5H30 post injection) were similar on Day -3 (baseline) in all treatment groups with a peak increase in C-peptide concentrations 2 hours after breakfast (T2H30) (Figure 51). On Day 56, the corrected C-peptide concentrations were lower compared to Day -3 values up to 3 hours postbreakfast (T3H30 post lixisenatide injection) but were slightly higher at 5 hours postbreakfast (T5H30 post lixisenatide injection). In both liraglutide 1.2 and 1.8 mg groups, corrected C-peptide concentrations were higher compared to Day-3 (baseline) up to 5 hours post-breakfast (T5H30) with a peak at 2 hours postbreakfast (T2H30 post IMP injection) in the liraglutide 1.2 mg group and at 3 hours postbreakfast (T3H30 post IMP injection) in the liraglutide 1.8 mg group.
L770: On Day -3, peak in corrected glucagon concentrations were observed approximately 30 minutes post-breakfast in all treatment groups (Figure 51). On Day 56, corrected glucagon concentrations were lower compared to baseline (Day -3) in all treatment groups during the first hour after breakfast ingestion (up to T1H30 post injection), with peak values observed at approximately 10 minutes postbreakfast (T0H40 post injection) in the lixisenatide group and at approximately 30 minutes post-breakfast (T1H post injection) in the liraglutide 1.2 and 1.8 mg groups.
L771: Figure 51: Mean (\pm SE) concentrations for corrected C-peptide and Glucagon on Day -3 and Day 56 – PD population
L772: Source eCTD module 5.3.4.2, Study PDY12625, Figure 9, page 115 and Figure 11, Page 120.
L773: After 8 weeks of treatment, both HbA_{1c} and body weight were decreased in the 3 treatment groups. Reductions from baseline in HbA_{1c} and body weight were comparable for lixisenatide 20 \mug and liraglutide 1.2 mg, while liraglutide 1.8 mg demonstrated greater reductions in HbA_{1c} and body weight compared to lixisenatide.
L774: *The results of this study provide useful insight into the mechanism of action of lixisenatide. However, the results for the initial 4.5 hour post-prandial glucose reduction were consistent with the observations from the single dose PK/PD study PDY12545. Sponsor used the data from this study to support the labeling claims for PPG reduction. However, in comparison to liraglutide, the effect of lixisenatide on PPG reduction seems limited to initial 4 hours and data does not demonstrate sustained reduction as observed for the liraglutide doses. The utility of the post-breakfast PPG reduction in the overall efficacy of lixisenatide is not fully understood.*

L776: ## 4 APPENDIX
L777: ## 4.1 Pharmacometric Review
L778: APPEARS THIS WAY ON ORIGINAL
L779: ## OFFICE OF CLINICAL PHARMACOLOGY:PHARMACOMETRIC REVIEW
L780: ## 1 Summary of Findings
L781: ## 1.1 Key Review Questions
L782: The purpose of this review is to address the following key questions.
L783: ## 1.1.1 Does the dose-response relationship for efficacy and safety support the proposed dose and dosing regimen of 20 µg once daily?
L784: Yes, the dose-response relationship of efficacy and safety supports the proposed dosing regimen of 20 µg once daily because of the following reasons:
L785: ## **Efficacy:**
L786: - In the dose ranging Phase 2 study, a clear dose response relationship was observed, where increasing doses from 5 µg to 30 µg, within the QD and BID-dosing regimen resulted in increase in response in terms of HbA1c (Figure 1).
- Compared to proposed 20 µg QD dosing, 10 µg BID-dosing regimen showed numerically slightly higher reduction in HbA1c. However, the added benefit with 10 µg BID-dosing should be weighed against narrow time window left for the administration of concomitant medication because of the effect of liraglutide on delaying gastric emptying time. Furthermore, as seen in Figure 2 cumulative distribution plots for change from baseline HbA1c is highly overlapped between the these two dosing regimens.
- Compared to 10 µg BID dose, 20 µg QD dose showed a numerically greater change in weight loss.
L787: ## **Safety**
L788: - In the dose ranging Phase 2 study, doses higher than 20 µg in both QD and BID dosing regimen resulted in more gastrointestinal related adverse events (Table 1).
- Additionally, compared to 20 µg QD dosing regimen 10 µg BID dosing regimen had less gastrointestinal related side effects. However, as stated above the added safety benefit with BID dosing regimen needs to be counterbalanced with the inconvenience of co-administration of other drugs.
L789: ## **Practicality**
L790: - Liraglutide delays the gastric emptying time and hence concomitant medications need to be administered (b) (4) 1 hr before liraglutide administration or with next meal for those drugs that have to be administered with food.
- Thus, with BID dosing regimen the subjects will have very little window for taking concomitant medication and hence we agree with the sponsor's proposed 20 µg QD dosing regimen as acceptable from a practical standpoint.
L791: Furthermore, in all the Phase 3 trials sponsor had only studied 20 µg QD dosing making it difficult to compare QD and BID doses beyond this 13 week Phase 2 trial.
L792: Summarized below are the dose-response relationships of efficacy and safety in detail.
L793: ## **Dose-Response Analysis for Efficacy**
L794: The sponsor studied only one dose of 20 µg QD in all the Phase 3 trials. Also, the trough concentrations data in the Phase 3 trials were below the limit of quantification. Thus, dose/exposure response relationship for safety and efficacy is limited to data available from a single Phase 2 trial of 13-week duration (Study #DRI6012).
L795: The selection of 20 µg QD dose in all the Phase 3 trials, was primarily based on a Phase 2 dose ranging study of 13-week treatment duration (Study #DRI6012). In this Phase 2 study, subjects were randomized to receive 5 µg, 10 µg, 20 µg, and 30 µg of lixisenatide dose as QD and BID dosing regimen. The primary efficacy endpoint for this Phase 2 study was change from baseline in HbA_{1c} at week 13. As shown in Figure 1, a clear dose-response relationship was observed, where increasing doses within the QD and BID-dosing regimen resulted in increase in response in terms of HbA_{1c}. Compared to proposed 20 µg QD dosing, 10 µg BID-dosing regimen showed slight numerically greater reduction in HbA_{1c} (-0.69 with 20 µg QD vs. -0.78 with 10 µg BID-dosing). Furthermore, Figure 2 shows cumulative distribution function (CDF) plots for change from baseline HbA_{1c} between these two dosing regimens. Overlapping CDF plots between 20 µg QD and 10 µg BID dosing indicates that the distribution for change from base line in HbA_{1c} is similar across both the dosing group (Figure 2).
L796: **Figure 1: Mean change in HbA_{1c} at endpoint (ITT population) (Study DRI6012)**
L797: BID = twice daily; HbA_{1c} = glycosylated hemoglobin; ITT = intent-to-treat; LS = least square; n = number of patients; QD = once daily
L798: Source: Sponsor's Summary of Clinical Efficacy report Pg 131
L799: **Figure 2: Cumulative distribution function plot for mean change from baseline in HbA1c at week 13 (Study DRI6012).**
L800: AVE0010 10 µg BID = Lixisenatide 10 µg BID dosing; AVE0010 20 µg QD = Lixisenatide 20 µg QD dosing
L801: Figure 3, represents the dose-response relationship for secondary pharmacodynamic endpoints: A) fasting plasma glucose (FPG) and B) 2-hr post prandial glucose (PPG) at week 13. A clear dose response relationship was observed for both secondary endpoints, where increasing doses within the QD and BID-dosing regimen resulted in increase in response. Compared to proposed 20 µg QD dosing, 10 µg BID-dosing regimen showed numerically greater reduction in FPG whereas for PPG 20 µg QD dosing seemed to be numerically better than 10 µg BID dosing. Lixisenatide has a short plasma half life of around 2-4 hours. Thus, better control of FPG is expected with BID dosing of lixisenatide since BID dosing can provide glucose control at both morning and evening meal resulting in better FPG with BID dosing. In addition, better effect on PPG with 20 µg QD dose is expected since with QD dosing 20 µg of dose is given at once before meal and hence more effect on PPG as compared to BID dosing where the 20 µg dose is divided into morning and evening dose of 10 µg each.
L802: **Figure 3: Mean change from baseline in A) fasting plasma glucose B) 2 hour post prandial glucose (Study DRI6012)**
L803: BID = twice daily; n = number of patients; QD = once daily
L804: Source: Sponsor's Summary of Clinical Pharmacology report Pg 118
L805: **Dose-Response relationship for Weight Loss:** A clear dose-response relationship for weight loss was observed for each dosing regimen, i.e., the higher the lixisenatide dose within a regimen (QD or BID) the larger the mean decrease in body weight. Figure 4 shows the mean (\pmSE) change in body weight by time and by treatment group. The data for mean change in weight (kg) from baseline to endpoint (week 13) is presented in Table 1. Compared to 10 \mug BID dose, 20 \mug QD dose has a greater weight loss change.

L810: ## Dose Response relationship for safety
L811: - **Gastrointestinal Disorder:** A clear dose-response relationship within both regimens was seen in the number of subjects with GI related adverse events. The frequencies for GI side effects ranged from 21.8% (5 µg) to 51.9% (30 µg) in lixisenatide QD groups and from 22.6% (5 µg) to 57.4% (30 µg) in lixisenatide BID groups. Doses higher than 20 µg in both QD and BID dosing regimen resulted in more gastrointestinal related adverse events (Table 2). Additionally, compared to 20 µg QD dosing regimen 10 µg BID dosing regimen has less gastrointestinal related side effects.

L817: In summary, a clear dose response was observed for efficacy and GI safety. Increasing doses within QD and BID dosing regimen resulted in increase in primary efficacy parameter, i.e. change from baseline in HbA1c. Secondary efficacy parameter such as fasting plasma glucose and post prandial glucose also showed dose response relationship. Similar trend of dose response was observed for GI related safety. Increasing dose in both QD and BID dosing regimen resulted in higher GI related incidences. Doses higher than 20 µg in both QD and BID dosing regimen resulted in more gastrointestinal related adverse events and thus not tested in Phase 3 program
L818: ## 1.1.2 Does the dose-response relationship for efficacy and safety supports the proposed 1-step dosing recommendation of starting dose of 10 µg QD for 14 days followed by maintenance dose of 20 µg QD?
L819: Yes, sponsor's proposal for 1-step dosing recommendation of starting dose of 10 µg QD for 14 days followed by maintenance dose of 20 µg QD is acceptable from efficacy and safety standpoint. The following reasons support our recommendations:
L820: - Similar Efficacy: In two Phase 3 studies, 1-step and 2-step dose increases resulted in similar efficacy (Figure 5).
- Similar GI Adverse Event Profile: Time to GI event for initial 30 days in two Phase 3 trials showed no difference in GI related adverse events in both the dosing regimen (Figure 6).
L821: In majority of the Phase 3 trials, the sponsor used a 2-step dosing regimen. In the 2-step dosing the patients were started with the initiation dose of 10 µg QD lixisenatide for 1 week followed by 15 µg QD for 1 week, and subsequently 20 µg QD as a maintenance dose. Later in the development phase sponsor used a 1-step dosing regimen, which is also the current proposed dosing recommendation in the product label. In the 1-step dosing regimen, patients were given 10 µg dose for 2 weeks followed by 20 µg QD maintenance dose. To evaluate the impact of 1-step vs. 2-step dose increase on efficacy and safety, sponsor conducted two Phase 3 trials.
L822: ## Effect of 1-Step vs 2-Step Dose Increase on Efficacy
L823: Sponsor's analysis of the 1-step and 2-step dose initiation in two Phase 3 studies, EFC6018 and EFC10743 is shown in Figure 5. Sponsor showed that a significant HbA1c reduction from baseline of similar magnitude with both dose increase regimens (-0.85% for the 1-step group and -0.73% for the 2-step group in Study EFC6018; -0.83% for the 2-step group and -0.92% for the 1-step group in Study EFC10743). Thus, based on sponsor's analysis, 1-step and 2-step dose initiation regimens were similar in terms of efficacy. Such similarity in results were expected since the only difference between the two groups was a higher dose (15 µg compared with 10 µg) during the second week of treatment.
L824: ## Effect of 1-Step vs 2-Step Dose Increase on Safety
L825: Survival plot for comparing time to GI event in two Phase 3 trials showed no difference in GI related adverse events in both the dosing regimen (Figure 6). We evaluated time to gastrointestinal adverse events in both 1-step and 2- step dosing regimen. The only difference in 1-step vs. 2-step dosing regimen is a higher dose 15 µg (compared with 10 µg in 1-step) during the second week of treatment. After the second week of treatment, both the dose groups had same maintenance dose of 20 µg QD. Survival plot for comparing time to GI event in two Phase 3 trials showed no significant differences in GI related adverse events in both the dosing regimen (Figure 6).

L827: Study EFC6018

L835: ## 1.1.3 What is the impact of Anti-drug antibody on efficacy?- Is it possible to identify the patient population early on that are likely to be non-responders?
L836: - Higher antibody concentrations resulted in attenuated HbA_{1c} response. Antibody concentration response analysis (Figure 7), showed a lower change from baseline in HbA_{1c} with increasing antibody concentrations based on week 4 and week 24 antibody concentrations.
- No correlation was observed between week 4 and week 24 antibody data (Figure 8). Since the week 4 antibody data is not predictive of week 24 antibody data, the analysis of antibody concentration to efficacy is exploratory in nature and cannot identify non responders early on with the drug treatment.
L837: ## Antibody Effect on Efficacy
L838: In Phase 1, Phase 2, and Phase 3 trials, sponsor observed formation of lixisenatide anti-drug antibody. To evaluate the potential neutralizing effect of anti-lixisenatide antibodies on efficacy, sponsor conducted a meta-analysis based on the pooled data from eight pivotal Phase 3 studies with at least 24-week treatment that collected the antibody data (Studies EFC6014, EFC6015, EFC6016, EFC6017, EFC10743, EFC10781, EFC10887 and EFC11321).
L839: Meta analysis results showed (Table 4) that at the end of the main treatment period (Week 24), anti-lixisenatide antibody status along with concomitant HbA_{1c} value was available in 1954 patients from the lixisenatide group, including 1333 (68.2%) patients assessed as antibody-positive, and 621 (31.8%) assessed as antibody-negative. Overall the LS mean change in HbA_{1c} from baseline was similar regardless of the antibody status (-0.82% [95% CI: -0.895 to -0.755] in antibody-positive patients and -0.83% [95% CI: -0.920 to -0.746] in antibody-negative patients).
L840: Overall, 1475 (78.0%) patients were either assessed as antibody negative or had an antibody concentration &lt;LLOQ (3.21 nmol/L), and the LS mean change in HbA_{1c} from baseline was -0.86% (95% CI: -0.930 to -0.795) in these patients. The LS mean change in HbA_{1c} from baseline was -0.64% (95% CI: -0.751 to -0.528) in the group of patients with a quantifiable antibody concentration \le 100 nmol/L, and it was -0.16% (95% CI: -0.418 to 0.096) in the group of 45 (2.4%) patients with antibody concentration &gt;100 nmol/L (Table 4).

L844: ## **Antibody Concentration Response Analysis on Efficacy**
L845: In several Phase 1 studies, antibody formation was observed as early as 3-5 weeks of treatment. Also, in several Phase 3 studies sponsor conducted anti-lixisenatide antibody measurements at week 4 of the treatment. We conducted an exposure (antibody concentration)-response analysis to evaluate the impact of antibody concentration at week 4 and week 24 on the efficacy at week 24. The main objective of this analysis was to identify patients upfront who are poor responder based on their antibody concentrations at week 4.
L846: Exposure (antibody concentration) response analysis, as seen in the Figure 7, showed a lower response in terms of change from baseline in HbA1c with increasing antibody concentrations at both week 4 and week 24.
L847: **Figure 7:** A) Percent change from baseline in HbA1c by antibody quartile at week 4. Based on pooled analysis of Phase 3 trials (N=275). B) Percent change from baseline in HbA1c by antibody concentration quartile at week 24. Based on Sponsor's data from pooled 8 Phase 3 trials (N=421)
L848: The antibody concentration is divided into four quartiles is denoted by the horizontal black line. Change from baseline in HbA1c is demonstrated as squares at the median of each quartile. The vertical bars represent the mean change from baseline in HbA1c with 95% confidence interval.
L849: We further looked in the relationship between week 4 antibody data to week 24 antibody data (Figure 8). No correlation was observed between week 4 and week 24 antibody data. Since the week 4 antibody data is not predictive of week 24 antibody data, the above analysis of antibody concentration to efficacy is exploratory in nature and no recommendation based on week 4 antibody concentration can be made at this point.
L850: Figure 8: Figure showing antibody concentrations at week 4 and at week 24. Red dashed vertical line and horizontal line represents the lower value of antibody concentration of fourth quartile at week 4 and week 24 respectively.
L851: Reviewer's comment: This analysis assumes that the bioanalytical method to detect the antibody concentrations was properly validated.
L852: ## 1.1.4 Is there a need for dose adjustment based on renal function?
L853: No dose adjustment is required for patients with mild or moderate renal function. The sponsor proposed not to use lixisenatide in patients with severe RI because of the very limited subject enrolled in the Phase 3 trials with severe RI. However, given that efficacy of lixisenatide is preserved in patients with moderate RI and that there is no unique safety concerns in this population, lixisenatide can be used with caution in patients with severe RI. The dose of lixisenatide should be restricted to 10 \mug QD in patients with severe RI, and should be increased to 20 \mug QD only in patients tolerating lixisenatide who require additional glycemic control. Please refer to Clinical Pharmacology review by Dr. Suryanarayana Sista and Clinical Review by Dr. Suchitra Balakrishnan for further details.
L854: **Effect of Renal Impairment of Efficacy:** Sponsor's subgroup analysis for efficacy based on renal function from the pooled data of nine Phase 3 placebo-controlled studies is shown in Table 5. The LS mean change from baseline in HbA_{1c} was -0.79 for normal renal function subjects, -0.92 for mild renally impaired subjects, and -0.79 for moderate renally impaired subjects (Table 5). The placebo adjusted difference in LS mean change from baseline in HbA_{1c} was -0.49 for normal renal function subjects, -0.62 for mild renally impaired subjects, and -0.80 for moderate renally impaired subjects (Table 5). In this pooled analysis there were very limited subjects with moderate (N=33) and none with severe renal function on lixisenatide treatment group. During the review cycle sponsor submitted data from an ongoing CV outcome study (ELIXA) which had a fair amount (N=215) of moderate renally impaired subjects on lixisenatide treatment group. Results on efficacy from ELIXA trial in renally impaired patient
L855: is shown in Table 6. The results of the ELIXA trial also showed that placebo adjusted difference in LS mean change from baseline in HbA_{1c} values for mild to moderate renally impaired patients were numerically greater compared to normal renal function subjects. Thus in terms of efficacy, the renal impairment patients showed better efficacy in pooled analysis and in ELIXA trial.

L874: **Effect of Renal Impairment on Safety:** Subgroup analysis for GI related side effects based on renal function on the pooled data of nine Phase 3 placebo-controlled studies is shown in Table 7. Safety results based on renal function indicates that more GI related side effects were observed in mild and moderate renally impaired patients as compared to normal. The increased number of GI related side effects could be due to higher PK exposure in renally impaired patients. There were no other unique safety concerns observed in patients with moderate renal impairment. No dosing adjustment is recommended in patients with moderate RI; however, use with caution is recommended because of general concerns of dehydration leading to acute renal failures with this class of drugs. Please refer to the clinical review by Dr. Suchitra Balakrishnan for complete details of the results from efficacy and safety trial patients with moderate RI.
L875: The sponsor proposed not to use lixisenatide in patients with severe RI because of the very limited subject enrolled in the Phase 3 trials with severe RI. However, given that efficacy of lixisenatide is preserved in patients with moderate RI and that there is no unique safety concerns in this population, lixisenatide can be used with caution in patients with severe RI. The dose of lixisenatide should be restricted to 10 µg QD in patients with severe RI, and should be increased to 20 µg QD only in patients tolerating lixisenatide who require additional glycemic control. The recommendation for 10 µg QD dose in patients with severe RI is based on exposure matching. Please refer to Clinical Pharmacology review by Dr. Suryanarayana Sista and Clinical Review by Dr. Suchitra Balakrishnan for further details.

L881: **Reviewers Comments:** *Higher number of patients in mild and moderate renally grouped patients experienced GI related side effects. Due to short half-life of this drug, the trough samples collected in the Phase 3 trials were below the limit of quantification and thus no exposure-response relationship could be done with these phase 3 trials. However, from the dedicated Phase 1 renal impairment study, it is known that mild and moderate subjects have higher exposure compared to subjects with normal renal function. Hence, more GI related side effect seen in mild and moderate group could perhaps be due to higher PK exposure in these subjects.*
L882: ## **Effect of Renal Impairment on Pharmacokinetics of Lixisenatide:**
L883: In dedicated Phase 1 trial, patients with mild moderate and severe renal impairment showed 48%, 65%, and 123% higher AUCs, respectively, as compared to subjects with normal renal function. Please refer to Clinical Pharmacology review by Dr. Suryanarayana Sista for further details.
L884: Similarly, in population PK analysis, creatinine clearance was found to be a significant covariate for lixisenatide clearance. Based on population PK analysis there is an increase in lixisenatide clearance with increasing creatinine clearance (Figures 12 A and B). Although creatinine clearance is identified as a covariate, the inclusion of creatinine clearance did not reduce the inter-individual variability on clearance. Therefore, the metrics for change in exposure in different renal impairment categories are primarily based on dedicated phase 1 trial.
L885: ## **1.1.5 Is there a need for dose adjustment for age, gender, race or bodyweight based on the pharmacokinetics of lixisenatide?**
L886: Based on the population PK analysis, no dose adjustment is required based on age, gender, race, and body weight. Based on the final model, no systematic trend was observed between inter-individual variability on clearance and body weight, age, gender or race (Figure 9). It is worthwhile to note the sponsor conducted dedicated Phase 1 trials to see the impact of age and race on the pharmacokinetics of lixisenatide. Population PK analysis to support labeling claims related to age and race is supportive to these dedicated Phase 1 trials.
L887: Figure 9: Based on Final Model, Inter-individual variability on clearance vs A) Race, B) Body Weight, C) Age, D) Gender
L888: ## 1.2 Recommendations
L889: Division of Pharmacometrics finds the NDA approvable from a clinical pharmacology perspective.
L890: ## 1.3 Labeling Recommendations
L891: The following are the labeling recommendations relevant to clinical pharmacology for NDA 204961. The red-strikeout-font is used to show the proposed text to be deleted and underline blue font to show text to be included or comments communicated to the sponsor.
L892: ## Effects of Age, Body weight, Gender and Race
L893: (b) (4)
L894: (b) (4)
L895: ## *Reviewer's comments:*
L896: (b) (4)
L897: *Proposed labeling claim by the Sponsor that*
L898: *is acceptable. However, details of the population PK analysis have been removed to keep the label concise.*
L899: ## 2 Results of Sponsor's Analysis
L900: ## 2.1 Population PK Analysis
L901: Sponsor performed population PK modeling utilizing data from 7 clinical studies that includes two Phase 1 (BDR6864 and POP6053), two Phase 2 study in T2DM subjects (PDY6797 and DRI6012), and three Phase 3 trials in T2DM subjects (EFC6018, EFC6015 and EFC10887). Primary objective of the population PK analysis was to identify subject characteristics (age, race, gender, renal function, body weight etc.) that are predictive of variability in the pharmacokinetic parameters of lixisenatide and therefore may require dose adjustments.
L902: ## 2.1.1 Methods
L903: PK data was collected from a total 1223 subjects (a total of approx 9600 plasma total lixisenatide concentrations), measured in the absence of anti-lixisenatide antibodies, from 75 healthy subjects and 1148 patients with T2DM. Data consisted of two Phase 1 (BDR6864 and POP6053), two Phase 2 study in T2DM subjects (PDY6797 and DRI6012), and three Phase 3 trials in T2DM subjects (EFC6018, EFC6015 and EFC10887). In these trials lixisenatide was administered as either single subcutaneous (n=75), or multiple subcutaneous doses (n=1148) ranging from scheduled 5 to 30 µg and was given QD or BID for up to 19 months. Description of the studies with other relevant information is provided in Table 8.
L904: **Table 8: Studies Used for the Population PK Model**
L905: Study no.	Study population	Dosesa	Pharmacokinetic sampling times
Phase 1 studies
BDR6864	Overweight and obese, otherwise healthy	10 µg	intensive 3d
POP6053	Healthy with either normal renal function or mild, moderate, or severe renal impairment	5 µg	intensive 3d
Phase 2 studies
PDY6797	T2DM	Starting doses of 5 µg or 10 µg, then weekly dose escalation in 5 µg increments from 5 or 10 µg QD or BID up to 30 µg QD or BID	intensive 2d
DRI6012	T2DM	QD or BID 5, 10, 20, or 30 µg for 13 weeks; for dose levels 20 and 30 µg: Starting dose: 5 µg for QD or BID 5 µg, to be increased in weekly increments of 5 µg	intensive over 4 hours after 13 weeks
Phase 3 studies
EFC6018	T2DM	20 µg QD	sparseb
EFC10887	Asian T2DM	20 µg QD	sparsec
EFC6015	T2DM	20 µg QD	sparsed
L906: *Source: Sponsor's Population PK Report, Page 357*
L907: A one-compartment pharmacokinetic model with first-order absorption was used to describe lixisenatide pharmacokinetics, using NONMEM Version 7.2 software. All runs were performed using the first-order conditional estimation method with \eta-\epsilon interaction. A combined additive and proportional statistical error model (in the log domain) was evaluated.
L908: PK data were fitted using NONMEM Version 7.2 ((ICON Development Solutions, Ellicott City, MD, USA). ADVAN2 subroutine and the first-order conditional estimation method with interaction (FOCEI) were used after logarithmic data transformation. Graphical data visualization, evaluation of NONMEM outputs, construction of goodness-of-fit plots and graphical model comparisons were conducted using R, version 2.12.1. Lixisenatide concentration data obtained in the presence of anti-lixisenatide antibodies were not considered for PopPK analysis. The concentrations were logarithmically transformed prior to modeling.
L909: The PPK model was developed in 3 stages. First, a base model was developed to describe the PK without consideration of covariate effects. Second, a full-covariate model was developed by incorporating the effects of all potentially significant covariate-parameter relationships, and lastly the final model was developed by retaining only the statistically significant covariate-parameter relationships by step-wise backward elimination. Model building and covariate assessments were conducted using standard methods. Covariate relationships were considered only on parameters on which inter-individual (IIV) and/or inter-occasion variability (IOV) were significant. The final model for the PK database was evaluated for performance using visual predictive check (VPC) and quantitative predictive check evaluation.
L910: ## 2.1.2 Final Model
L911: To arrive at the final model, the full model was subjected to a step-wise backward elimination procedure. The pharmacokinetics of lixisenatide following subcutaneous injection was best described by a one-compartment model, with a first order absorption (k_a), with IIV and IOV on apparent clearance (CL/F), apparent volume of distribution (V/F) and mean absorption time (MAT=1/k_a), and with a combined additive and proportional residual error (in the log domain).
L912: After the backwards elimination procedure was completed, 3 covariate-parameter relationships were retained in the final model: cCrCL on CL/F, and body weight on V/F and dose on MAT. The IIV parameters of the base model were specified by a lognormal distribution. The NONMEM output for the final model is presented in Table 9.
L913: **Table 9: Parameter Estimates of Final Model (Final)**
L914: Structural model:
CL/F = \theta_{CL/F} \cdot (1 + \theta_{CLCR\_CL} \cdot (CLCR - 6.2 \text{ L/h})) \cdot e^{\eta_{CL\_IIV} + \eta_{CL\_IOV}}
V/F = \theta_{V/F} \cdot (\frac{WT}{82.1 \text{ kg}})^{\theta_{WT\_V}} \cdot e^{\eta_{V\_IIV} + \eta_{V\_IOV}}
MAT = \theta_{MAT} \cdot (\frac{\text{Dose}}{15 \text{ } \mu\text{g}})^{\theta_{\text{Dose\_MAT}}} \cdot e^{\eta_{MAT\_IIV}}
Residual random effect model:
\log(y(t)) = \log(f(t) + m) + \frac{f(t)}{f(t)+m} \cdot e_1(t) + \frac{m}{f(t)+m} \cdot e_2(t)

L922: ## 2.1.2.1 Lixisenatide Covariate Effects
L923: After incorporating the covariates into the basic model, the IIV on CL/F increased from 35.5 % to 40.3 %, and the IIV for MAT only decreased from 33.2% to 30.0%, whereas the IIV for V/F clearly decreased from 61.4% to 30.3% (all values in log domain). Thus, there were essentially no relevant reductions in the IIV for CL/F and MAT by incorporating covariates.
